React + TypeScript: Build a Modern Web App · Participant Workbook

Part 1: TypeScript and React Foundations

Sessions 1 to 9. Modules 1 to 3: why React and TypeScript belong together and a working local workspace (Node LTS, VS Code, the shoreline-api mock back end, a Vite react-ts app), the TypeScript you need before React (types and inference, object types and unions, functions and generics, typing real API data), then React in TypeScript from the first component to props, lists, state and events, forms with actions, styling and accessible components. Runs on its own as a short course and ends with the Part 1 project check-in and assessment.

Instructor Kit · Participant Workbook

Session 1: Why React + TypeScript and Your Workspace

Your workbook for Session 1: the environment check, a diagnostic, the key ideas of Lessons 1.1 and 1.2, worked examples, the guided labs, predict-then-check practice, a self-check for each lesson, and your exit ticket.

Lessons 1.1–1.2 Session 1 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Find out whether your computer is ready: the environment check ends with READY, and you know what to do if it does not.
  • Explain what React does and what TypeScript does, when TypeScript checks and what it cannot catch.
  • Read a TypeScript error like a developer: file, line and column, the code, the message, the follow-up line.
  • Run your workspace: Node LTS, VS Code, the course API on :4000 and Shoreline Crew HQ on :5173, each in its own terminal.
  • Tell running apart from checking: the dev server runs; your editor, npx tsc -b and npm run build check; npm run lint looks for suspicious code.

Start Here: Is Your Computer Ready?

Environment check
  • ☐ Node.js LTS (24 or newer) and VS Code are installed; node -v in a NEW terminal starts with v24 or more
  • ☐ My course folder shoreline holds shoreline-api (unzipped, server.ts directly inside), shoreline-hq (created with npm create vite@latest, npm install done) and the course's labs/ folder
  • ☐ Terminal 1: cd shoreline-api, then node server.ts (left running)
  • ☐ Terminal 2, in shoreline: node labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/check_environment.mjs
  • ☐ The last line says READY for React + TypeScript: Build a Modern Web App (or READY WITH NOTES)
  • ☐ If it said NOT READY, I wrote my plan here (from the install clinic) and will finish it before Session 2:

Five messages to know by heart: node is not recognized = open a new terminal (or install Node) · ERR_UNKNOWN_FILE_EXTENSION = your Node is too old · nothing answers at http://localhost:4000 = start the API · Could not read package.json = wrong folder, cd shoreline-hq · port 4000 is already in use = the API is already running in another terminal.

Diagnostic: predict, then check

Six questions on the JavaScript this course builds on: arrays and nested objects, map and filter, promises, template literals and the terminal. Circle your answer before the class reveals it. Nobody collects this.

Missed one? The skill and where it comes back: D1 arrays (every list of cleanups from Lesson 3.3 on (map, keys)) · D2 objects inside objects (Lesson 2.2 (object types) and every component that reads a cleanup's site) · D3 filter and map (Lesson 2.3 (typed functions over lists) and lists in Lesson 3.3) · D4 promises (Lesson 4.1 (effects and fetching) and every request after it) · D5 template literals and undefined (Lesson 1.1 (the NaN Node printed) and Lesson 2.1 (inference)) · D6 folders and the terminal (Lesson 1.2 (two terminals, the right folder) and every npm command after it). For practice at home: labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/diagnostic_starter.mjs has JavaScript answer D1, D2 and D3, one small mistake each.

D1
What does this print?
const sites = ['Driftwood Cove', 'North Jetty', 'Seal Rock Beach']
console.log(sites.length, sites[0])
A) 3 Driftwood Cove
B) 3 North Jetty
C) 2 Driftwood Cove
D) 3 Seal Rock Beach
D2
What does this print?
const cleanup = { title: 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', site: { name: 'Driftwood Cove', town: 'Port Marisol' } }
console.log(cleanup.site)
A) Driftwood Cove
B) undefined
C) { name: 'Driftwood Cove', town: 'Port Marisol' }
D) An error
D3
What does this print?
const events = [
  { title: 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', capacity: 18, spotsTaken: 18 },
  { title: 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean', capacity: 10, spotsTaken: 9 },
]
console.log(events.filter((e) => e.spotsTaken < e.capacity).map((e) => e.title))
A) [ 'Weekend Cove Cleanup' ]
B) [ 9 ]
C) [ 'Weekend Cove Cleanup', 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean' ]
D) [ 'Jetty Rocks Deep Clean' ]
D4
With the API running, what does this print?
const response = fetch('http://localhost:4000/api/events')
console.log(response)
A) Promise { <pending> }
B) The JSON of the cleanups
C) undefined
D) Nothing: it waits for the answer
D5
What does this print?
const event = { capacity: 18, spotsTaken: 12 }
console.log(`${event.capcity - event.spotsTaken} spots left`)
A) 6 spots left
B) NaN spots left
C) undefined spots left
D) An error: capcity is not defined
D6

A) node check_environment.mjs
B) cd shoreline, then node labs/module-01/l1_0-orientation/check_environment.mjs
C) npm run check_environment
D) sudo node labs/check_environment.mjs

Lessons

1.1

Why React + TypeScript

By the end you can
  • Explain in your own words what React does and what TypeScript does, and why teams use them together
  • Describe when TypeScript's checks happen (while you write and build) and what they cannot catch (data that arrives while the app runs)
  • Read a TypeScript error message and find the line, the cause and the suggested fix
  • Write an interface for a piece of real API data and use it to type a function
  • Connect the screens of Shoreline Crew HQ to the API data each one needs
Key ideas
  • React turns data into screens with components and keeps them up to date: you describe what the screen should be (declarative), React works out the changes.
  • TypeScript is JavaScript plus types: short descriptions of what a value may be. interface CleanupEvent { ... } describes a shape; : CleanupEvent, event: CleanupEvent and : number are annotations.
  • Checking happens before running: in your editor as you type and when tsc runs. The types then disappear: browsers and Node run plain JavaScript.
  • Running is not checking: node file.ts and Vite's dev server strip the types and run the code without checking it.
  • Reading an error: file:line:column, the code (TS2551, TS2322, TS2741), the message (often with a suggestion), the line, then the indented follow-up that says where TypeScript's knowledge came from.
  • What TypeScript cannot do: see data that arrives while the app runs (Lesson 2.4, Module 8), check logic (tests, Module 9), check anything typed any. A union of string literals ('draft' | 'scheduled' | ...) allows exactly those words.
Worked example: one file, two tools

The lesson's cleanup script with one typo (capcity), handled by two tools. Node runs it:

node spots.ts
Weekend Cove Cleanup: NaN spots left

The TypeScript compiler checks it:

npx tsc --noEmit spots.ts
spots.ts:18:16 - error TS2551: Property 'capcity' does not exist on type 'CleanupEvent'. Did you mean 'capacity'?

18   return event.capcity - event.spotsTaken
                  ~~~~~~~

  spots.ts:5:3 - 'capacity' is declared here.
    5   capacity: number
        ~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in spots.ts:18

Read it in four moves. Where: spots.ts:18:16 = file, line 18, column 16. What: the code TS2551 and the message, with a suggestion (Did you mean 'capacity'?). The line: printed with ~~~~ under the problem. Why it knows: the indented follow-up, spots.ts:5:3 - 'capacity' is declared here: the interface you wrote. Node printed NaN because it removed the types and ran what was left; nobody checked.

Guided lab
  • Open labs/module-01/l1_1-why-react-typescript/TASKS.md and fixtures/events.json first: the JSON is what your interface must describe.
  • A terminal in the starter folder: npm install once, then npm test (five failures with TODO) and npm run check (four errors).
  • Task 1: CleanupEvent and summary(event) in events.ts. Run npm test after each change; two tests pass when it is right.
Task 2 errorLineCodeThe cause, in my words
1
2
3
  • Done when npm test says ℹ fail 0 and npm run check prints nothing after its two > lines. Finished early? Task 3, then the stretch.
Predict, then check

Write both predictions first, then run them in a folder with TypeScript installed (the lab's starter works if you remove its tsconfig.json in a copy) or in the Playground (Run, and the Errors tab). A wrong prediction you can explain afterward is worth more than a right guess.

P1
Predict what node prints and whether tsc accepts it, then run both. (A number minus a string.)
const capacity = 18
const spotsTaken = '12'
console.log(capacity - spotsTaken)
P2
Predict what node prints and whether tsc accepts it, then run both. (A string plus a number.)
const capacity = '18'
console.log(capacity + 2)

9 const cleanup: CleanupEvent = {
        ~~~~~~~

  spots.ts:6:3 - 'spotsTaken' is declared here.
    6   spotsTaken: number
        ~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in spots.ts:9
A) Delete spotsTaken from the interface on line 6
B) Change CleanupEvent to any on line 9

B) Writing capacity: '18' where the interface says number

console.log(current)
A) Nothing: 'canceled' is a string, and EventStatus is made of strings

C) error TS2551: Property 'canceled' does not exist

1.2

Your Workspace

By the end you can
  • Install the current long-term-support version of Node.js and VS Code, and check that they work from a terminal
  • Run the Shoreline Crew API locally and read its answers in a browser
  • Create a Vite react-ts project, install its packages and run its dev server
  • Explain what each file in a new project does and how index.html, main.tsx and App.tsx connect
  • Tell running apart from checking: use your editor, tsc -b, npm run lint and npm run build to find mistakes the dev server lets through
Key ideas
  • Two things on your computer: Node.js LTS (24 or newer, with npm) and VS Code. Every other tool is installed per project by npm install, at the versions in package.json.
  • One course folder, shoreline: shoreline-api (downloaded, never edited), shoreline-hq (your app) and later ts-lab. Not in OneDrive, iCloud or Dropbox.
  • Two terminals while you work: the API (node server.ts in shoreline-api, http://localhost:4000) and the dev server (npm run dev in shoreline-hq, http://localhost:5173). Start the API first.
  • How the page starts: index.html (div id=root) loads src/main.tsx, which calls createRoot(...).render(<App />); App.tsx is the first component.
  • The scripts: dev runs (no checking); build = tsc -b && vite build (a type error stops it); lint = oxlint (suspicious code, warnings do not fail it); preview serves the build.
  • Three places that check: the editor (red underline, Problems panel), npx tsc -b (silence = no errors) and npm run build. Aim for 0 lint warnings and a clean build at the end of every lesson.
Worked example: one typo, three places

One typo in App.tsx, {crewName.toUppercase()}, seen three ways. Your editor underlines it as you type. The dev server serves the page anyway, and the browser goes blank (the Console says crewName.toUppercase is not a function). The compiler names it before anything runs:

npx tsc -b
src/App.tsx:7:21 - error TS2551: Property 'toUppercase' does not exist on type 'string'. Did you mean 'toUpperCase'?

7       <h1>{crewName.toUppercase()} HQ</h1>
                      ~~~~~~~~~~~

  node_modules/@typescript/typescript-linux-x64/lib/lib.es5.d.ts:506:5 - 'toUpperCase' is declared here.
    506     toUpperCase(): string;
            ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/App.tsx:7

And npm run build stops at its first step, because the script is tsc -b && vite build: no vite build line ever appears. Fixed, npx tsc -b prints nothing at all, and the build ends like this (times and hashes differ on every machine):

> shoreline-hq@0.0.0 build
> tsc -b && vite build

vite v8.3.8 building client environment for production...
✓ 16 modules transformed.
computing gzip size...
dist/index.html                   0.46 kB │ gzip:  0.30 kB
dist/assets/index-DzE5JpJi.css    0.27 kB │ gzip:  0.18 kB
dist/assets/index-B1j6Mmhu.js   219.66 kB │ gzip: 68.65 kB

✓ built in …ms

The editor told you while you typed, the build refused to ship it, and the dev server let a user find it. That is the whole lesson in one typo.

Guided lab
  • Open labs/module-01/l1_2-your-workspace/TASKS.md. A terminal in starter: npm install once.
  • Task 1: npm test (a crash), then npm run check (the same mistake, named before it runs). Fix it.
  • Task 2: the tests pass; npm run check still has two errors. Fix the annotation, not the value. Task 3: npm run lint.
QuestionYour answer
Which tool found the typo first? Which one would a user have found it with?
Did the tests notice the wrong annotation in Task 2? Why not?
Which tools reported the unused constant, and which of them fails a build?

Glossary

TermMeaning
ReactA library that draws the screen from your data with components and keeps it up to date
componentA function that returns what a part of the screen should look like (App, later CleanupCard)
TypeScriptJavaScript plus types, checked before the code runs; the types are removed afterwards
type, annotationA description of what a value may be / writing one after a name (capacity: number)
interfaceA named description of an object's shape (interface CleanupEvent { ... })
tscThe TypeScript compiler; npx tsc -b checks the project, --noEmit checks a file without writing JavaScript
static checkingChecking code before it runs (editor, tsc), as opposed to while it runs
union of string literalsA type that allows only some words: 'draft' | 'scheduled' | 'completed' | 'cancelled'
any"Stop checking this value": the course avoids it
Node.js / npmJavaScript outside the browser / its package manager and script runner
LTSLong-term support: the Node release line tools test against (24 or newer)
shoreline-apiThe course's mock back end with sample data, at http://localhost:4000/api
Vite, dev serverThe build tool that creates, serves (:5173) and builds the app; the dev server runs code without checking it
npm run buildtsc -b && vite build: check every file, then write the finished site to dist/
oxlint, linterA tool that flags suspicious code (npm run lint); warnings do not fail it
HMRHot module replacement: Vite swaps the changed piece of the running page when you save
portThe number that says which running program a request is for (4000 the API, 5173 the dev server)
Instructor Kit · Participant Workbook

Session 2: Types, Inference and Shaping Data

Your workbook for Session 2: a warm-up, the key ideas of Lessons 2.1 and 2.2, worked examples, the guided labs, predict-then-check practice, the project checklists, a self-check for each lesson and the exit ticket.

Lessons 2.1–2.2 Session 2 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Set up ts-lab, your TypeScript workbench, and use its rhythm: save, npm run check, read, node file.ts.
  • Predict what TypeScript infers, and annotate only the edges: parameters, late values, empty arrays, outside data.
  • Treat outside data as unknown and check it with typeof; handle null and undefined before using a value.
  • Describe objects with interfaces, optional and literal-union properties, and choose between interface and type.
  • Narrow unions (typeof, equality, a discriminant, in) and handle "maybe nothing" with if, ?? and ?..

Warm-up: Session 1 Retrieval

Answer from memory, then compare with a partner.

W1
node spots.ts printed NaN; npx tsc --noEmit spots.ts printed TS2551. Why the difference?
W2
npx tsc -b prints nothing at all in shoreline-hq. Good or bad?
W3
npm run build printed error TS2322. Did vite build run?
W4
Which tool flags an unused constant as a warning, and which one makes it an error?

Lessons

2.1

Types and Inference

By the end you can
  • Set up a TypeScript workbench (ts-lab) with its own package.json, tsconfig.json and a check script
  • Predict the type TypeScript infers for a value, including the difference between let and const
  • Decide when to write a type annotation: parameters, values that arrive later, and empty arrays
  • Use the basic types (string, number, boolean, null, undefined) and typed arrays
  • Explain why unknown is safer than any for data from outside your program, and check an unknown value with typeof
  • Read the errors TS2322, TS2345, TS7006, TS18046 and TS18047 and know what each one is asking you to fix
Key ideas
  • ts-lab is a small Node project next to the app: its own TypeScript (--save-dev --save-exact), a strict tsconfig, npm run check = tsc --noEmit. node file.ts runs; npm run check checks every .ts file in the folder.
  • Inference: TypeScript reads the value. let gets a general type (number); const gets a literal type (18, "Shoreline Crew"). Inferred types are checked as strictly as written ones.
  • Annotate the edges: parameters (TS7006 otherwise), values that arrive later, empty arrays, outside data. Return types and callback parameters are usually inferred.
  • Basic types are lowercase: string, number, boolean, null, undefined; arrays string[] (= Array<string>). With strict checks, null is its own type.
  • any vs unknown: both accept anything; any checks nothing (and spreads), unknown must be narrowed (for example typeof x === 'string') before use. Outside data is unknown.
  • Errors are questions: TS2322 (value does not fit), TS2345 (argument does not fit), TS7006 (parameter needs a type), TS18046 (check the unknown), TS18047/18048 (handle nothing).
Worked example: any lets it through, unknown asks first

The lesson's tagLine typo, with JSON.parse returning any. The check has nothing to say:

> tsc --noEmit

and Node crashes (trimmed):

                         ^

scratch.ts:4:13 - error TS18046: 'meta' is of type 'unknown'.

4 console.log(meta.tagLine.toUpperCase())
              ~~~~


Found 1 error in scratch.ts:4

TS18046: "you cannot use this value until you have checked what it is". Inside if (typeof saved === 'string') the value is a string, and the check is silent. Outside data is unknown until checked.

2.2

Shaping Data

By the end you can
  • Describe objects with object types and interfaces, including nested objects, and read the errors for missing, misspelled and extra properties
  • Choose between interface and type, and build one shape on another with extends or &
  • Mark properties optional (?) or read-only (readonly) and say what each promise means for the code that uses them
  • Model "one of these" with unions and literal types, such as a cleanup's four statuses
  • Narrow a union with typeof, equality, in and a discriminant property, and check an unknown value that should be an object
  • Handle null and undefined with an if, ?? and ?., and know when the ! assertion is honest
Key ideas
  • Object types and interfaces: list each property and its type. Object literals are checked for missing (TS2741), misspelled (TS2561, with a suggestion) and extra (TS2353) properties.
  • interface or type: both describe objects; only type names a union. Build on shapes with extends (interface) or & (type). Course convention: interface for objects, type for the rest.
  • Optional ? reads as T | undefined everywhere; readonly forbids changing a property after creation (a check, not a lock).
  • Unions: A | B is "one of these"; a union of literal types ('sand' | 'rocky' | 'marsh' | 'mixed') is a fixed list. Annotate objects where you create them, or their properties widen to string.
  • Narrowing: typeof, ===/switch, a discriminant property (kind), in, and the object check typeof x === 'object' && x !== null && 'name' in x.
  • Maybe nothing: if, ?? (not ||: 0 and '' are real values), ?. (then ??); ! only for what your own project guarantees.
Worked example: the widening trap

The widening trap: the object is built first and typed later.

type Terrain = 'sand' | 'rocky' | 'marsh' | 'mixed'

interface Site {
  name: string
  terrain: Terrain
}

function describeSite(site: Site) {
  return `${site.name} (${site.terrain})`
}

const flats = { name: 'Sandpiper Flats', terrain: 'marsh' }
console.log(describeSite(flats))
> ts-lab@1.0.0 check
> tsc --noEmit

scratch.ts:13:26 - error TS2345: Argument of type '{ name: string; terrain: string; }' is not assignable to parameter of type 'Site'.
  Types of property 'terrain' are incompatible.
    Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'Terrain'.

13 console.log(describeSite(flats))
                            ~~~~~


Found 1 error in scratch.ts:13

Read it from the bottom: terrain was inferred as string (a const object's properties can change), and a string is not a Terrain. Say what the object is where you create it, const flats: Site = { … }, and it prints:

Sandpiper Flats (marsh)

Glossary

TermMeaning
ts-labThe course's TypeScript workbench: a small Node project next to the app (Module 2)
type inferenceTypeScript working out a type from a value, an expression or the context
literal typeA type that is one exact value: 18, "Shoreline Crew", 'draft'
annotationA type you write after a name: capacity: number
any / unknownAccepts anything and checks nothing / accepts anything but must be checked before use
narrowingA check (typeof, ===, in, a discriminant) that makes a type more precise inside its branch
strict modeThe full set of strict checks (no implicit any, strict null); on by default in TypeScript 7
object type, interfaceA description of an object's properties / a named one
type aliastype Name = …: a name for any type, unions included
optional propertycheckInCode?: string: may be absent; reads as string | undefined
union typeA | B: a value that is one of the members
discriminated unionA union of object types told apart by one shared literal property (the discriminant, kind)
?? / ?.Nullish coalescing (a fallback for null/undefined) / optional chaining (stop at null/undefined)
! (non-null assertion)A promise that a value is not null or undefined, which TypeScript does not check
excess property checkTypeScript's check for properties an object literal has but its type does not (TS2353, TS2561)

Lessons

2.3

Functions and Generics

By the end you can
  • Annotate a function's parameters and return type, and decide when an explicit return type is worth writing
  • Use optional, default and rest parameters and an options object, and read the errors for missing or misplaced arguments
  • Describe a function as a value with a function type, such as (event: CleanupEvent) => string, and pass typed callbacks
  • Write generic functions and types (first<T>, Page<T>) and let TypeScript infer the type arguments
  • Constrain a type parameter with extends so a generic function can use the properties it needs
  • Read overloaded and generic declarations in TypeScript's own library, such as querySelector and find
Key ideas
  • A function's contract: a type after each parameter, one after the parentheses for the return. TS2554 counts arguments; TS2345 checks each one. Write return types on functions other code relies on; void for functions that only print or save.
  • Optional x? (inside: T | undefined), default x = 'spots' (optional for the caller, never undefined inside), rest ...counts: number[]. Optional parameters go last (TS1016).
  • An options object destructured with defaults ({ showStatus = false }: LineOptions = {}) handles many options; a misspelled option is TS2561. React props work exactly like this.
  • Function types: type EventFormatter = (event: CleanupEvent) => string. Callbacks get their parameter types from the context; a function with fewer parameters fits.
  • Generics: first<T>(items: T[]): T | undefined; T is inferred from the argument. Generic types: Page<T>, Array<T>, Map<K, V>, later Promise<T> and useState<T>.
  • Constraints: T extends { id: string } lets the body use id and refuses items without one. TypeScript's own declarations (querySelector, find) read the same way.
Worked example: a generic and its constraint

first<T> reads its type from the argument, and keeps every check. With any, the misspelled .nmae passed the check and printed undefined; with the generic:

interface Site {
  id: string
  name: string
}

const sites: Site[] = [{ id: 'driftwood-cove', name: 'Driftwood Cove' }]
const bagCounts = [14, 9, 22]

function first<T>(items: T[]): T | undefined {
  return items[0]
}

const site = first(sites)
const count = first(bagCounts)
const nobody = first<string>([])

console.log(site?.name ?? 'No sites', count ?? 0, nobody ?? 'Nobody yet')
console.log(site?.nmae)
> ts-lab@1.0.0 check
> tsc --noEmit

scratch.ts:18:19 - error TS2339: Property 'nmae' does not exist on type 'Site'.

18 console.log(site?.nmae)
                     ~~~~


Found 1 error in scratch.ts:18

T became Site, so site?.nmae is a real error with a suggestion. And a constraint says what T must have: findById<T extends { id: string }> refuses a list without ids, at the call:

scratch.ts:22:22 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'number[]' is not assignable to parameter of type '{ id: string; }[]'.
  Type 'number' is not assignable to type '{ id: string; }'.
Found 1 error in scratch.ts:22

2.4

Typing Real Data

By the end you can
  • Share types between files with export and import type, and explain what verbatimModuleSyntax asks of you
  • Write type predicates (value is EventStatus) that turn unknown data into checked, typed data
  • Build new types from existing ones with Pick, Omit, Partial and Record, and read them in the API's real types
  • Read every line of the tsconfig files Vite created, and switch on noUncheckedIndexedAccess
  • Configure and type an environment variable (VITE_API_URL) and explain why front-end variables are never secret
  • Fetch cleanups from shoreline-api through a reusable, generic, checked client
Key ideas
  • Modules: export once, import type { … } from './shapes.ts' everywhere. verbatimModuleSyntax requires import type for types (TS1484): Node strips them, so there is nothing to import at run time.
  • Type guards: a return type value is EventStatus says what true means; TypeScript narrows with it like with typeof, and filter(guard) narrows the array. A guard is a promise TypeScript cannot check.
  • Object guards: isRecord(value) (an object, not null, not an array), then one check per property, chained with &&. Missing is not null.
  • Utility types: Pick<T, Keys>, Omit<T, Keys>, Partial<T>, Record<Keys, V> (every key required: lookup tables).
  • tsconfig: strict is the default in TypeScript 7; noUncheckedIndexedAccess makes items[0] a T | undefined (loops unaffected).
  • Environment variables: .env.example (in Git) and .env.local (yours); only VITE_ names reach the code, as text pasted into the build: nothing in the front end is secret. vite-env.d.ts types them.
Worked example: a boolean versus a type predicate

A type guard says what true means. With a plain boolean return type, the value inside the if is still unknown:

scratch.ts:11:25 - error TS18046: 'value' is of type 'unknown'.
Found 1 error in scratch.ts:11

With value is EventStatus, the same code checks clean and runs:

a status: SCHEDULED
not a status: finished
not a status: 42
[ 'draft', 'scheduled' ]

The last line is filter(isEventStatus): the result's type is EventStatus[]. The guard is a promise TypeScript cannot check: it must check every property its type promises.

  • Task 1: one word fixes the import. Task 2: a type predicate, then isImpactTotals for the whole answer.

Glossary

TermMeaning
return type, voidThe type a function promises to return / "returns nothing useful"
optional, default, rest parameterx?: T / x = value / ...xs: T[]
options objectOne object parameter destructured with defaults, instead of many positional ones
function type(event: CleanupEvent) => string: the type of a function value
callback, contextual typingA function passed to another / its parameter types read from where it is passed
generic, type parameterA function or type with a placeholder type T, filled in (usually inferred) at each use
constraintT extends { id: string }: what every T must have
overloadSeveral signatures for one function (querySelector, find): read, rarely written
import typeAn import of types only, removed before the code runs
type predicate, type guardA return type value is T / a function that has one
utility typeA generic type that builds a type from another: Pick, Omit, Partial, Record
noUncheckedIndexedAccessThe setting that makes items[0] a T | undefined
environment variable, .env.localA setting that changes between machines / Vite's file for this machine's values
import.meta.envWhere Vite puts the VITE_ variables in your code

Lesson

3.1

Components and TSX

By the end you can
  • Explain how React turns your component functions into what the browser shows, and why rendering must be pure
  • Write TSX: one parent element, closed tags, className and other camelCase attributes, and {expressions} in braces
  • Read the errors TypeScript reports for TSX, including attributes that do not exist and values React cannot draw
  • Write components as typed functions with named exports, one per file, and compose them into a tree
  • Import an image into a component and explain how TypeScript knows its type
  • Give Shoreline Crew HQ its shell: AppLayout, Header, Footer, a HomePage, the brand colors and the favicon
Key ideas
  • Declarative: a component says what the page should look like for today's data; React compares and changes only what differs (the tide chart). createRoot(...).render(<App />) starts it.
  • TSX becomes function calls (jsx('h1', { className, children })) that return plain-object elements: tags are values, attributes are object properties.
  • Rules: JavaScript's names (className, htmlFor, maxLength; aria-*/data-* keep dashes); one parent or a fragment <>…</>; close every tag; braces hold expressions, not statements; comments in {/* */}; {' '} keeps a space at a line end.
  • What React draws: strings, numbers, elements, arrays; true/false/null/undefined draw nothing; an object is TS2322 (not a ReactNode).
  • Components are capitalized functions returning TSX (inferred JSX.Element; no return annotation), one per file, named exports, function declarations. Lowercase = an HTML tag.
  • Pure rendering: same inputs, same TSX; no new Date() in the body (oxlint react(purity)); Strict Mode renders twice in development. Images: import logoUrl from '../assets/logo.svg' is a string (Vite's declaration); alt="" for decoration.
Worked example: an object in braces

The lesson's summary card with one line changed to <p>{site}</p>. The type check says:

src/Scratch.tsx:11:10 - error TS2322: Type '{ name: string; town: string; }' is not assignable to type 'ReactNode'.

11       <p>{site}</p>
            ~~~~~~

  node_modules/@types/react/index.d.ts:2361:9 - The expected type comes from property 'children' which is declared here on type 'DetailedHTMLProps<HTMLAttributes<HTMLParagraphElement>, HTMLParagraphElement>'
    2361         children?: ReactNode | undefined;
                 ~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:11

ReactNode is everything React can draw: text, numbers, elements, the nothing-values (true, false, null, undefined) and arrays of these. An object is not one of them: say which property to show, {site.name}, {site.town}. In the browser, without the check, this is a blank page and "Objects are not valid as a React child".

Session 5: Props, Children and Composition

Your workbook for Session 5: the key ideas of Lesson 3.2, a worked example of the three prop errors, the guided lab, predict-then-check practice, the lesson project's checklist, a self-check and your exit ticket.

Lesson 3.2 Session 5 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Explain props as the ONE object React passes to a component, destructured and described by an interface.
  • Read the three prop errors: TS2741 (missing), TS2322 "does not exist" (unknown), TS2322 "the expected type comes from" (wrong type).
  • Use optional props with defaults, children: ReactNode, and parts of the API's types (Site['image'], Pick).
  • Choose composition over a growing list of props, and keep props read-only.
  • Build the HQ's Beach sites page from a typed Site[].

Lesson

3.2

Props, Children and Composition

By the end you can
  • Explain props as the single object React passes to a component, and destructure it in the parameter list
  • Type props with an interface, and read the errors TypeScript gives for missing, misspelled and mistyped props
  • Make props optional with ? and give them defaults, the same way you did for options objects in Lesson 2.3
  • Accept content between a component's tags with children: ReactNode, and choose composition over a long list of props
  • Reuse the API's domain types in props, including a part of a type such as Site['image']
  • Build the HQ's sites page from a typed Site[] with Card, Badge, SitePicture and SiteCard, and give AppLayout its children
Key ideas
  • Props are one object. <SiteName name="Driftwood Cove" town="Port Marisol" /> calls SiteName({ name, town }). Destructure it in the parameter list and describe it with an interface (SiteNameProps) right above the component.
  • Strings in quotes, everything else in braces: town="Port Marisol", capacity={18}, site={site}, icon={<span>⭐</span>}.
  • Every use is checked: TS2741 for a missing prop, TS2322 for an unknown prop or a wrong type, each pointing back at the interface.
  • Optional props use ? plus a default in the destructuring (label = 'Low tide'); without the default the prop is string | undefined inside (TS18048). A boolean prop without a value is true. No defaultProps.
  • children: ReactNode lets a component wrap any content; a component that does not declare children accepts none. Compose small components instead of adding props for every case.
  • Reuse the API's types: site: Site, a part with Site['image'], a few properties with Pick<Volunteer, 'name' | 'phone'>. Props are read-only: react(immutability) warns when you change one.
Worked example: one component, three broken uses

The lesson's SiteName used three ways that break its contract. npx tsc -b (in shoreline-hq, with the snippet in src/Scratch.tsx):

export function NextCleanup() {
  return (
    <p>
      Next cleanup: <SiteName name="Driftwood Cove" town="Port Marisol" />
      <SiteName name="North Jetty" />
      <SiteName name="Otter Creek Mouth" twon="Seal Rock" />
      <SiteName name={42} town="Calder Bay" />
    </p>
  )
}
src/Scratch.tsx:18:8 - error TS2741: Property 'town' is missing in type '{ name: string; }' but required in type 'SiteNameProps'.

18       <SiteName name="North Jetty" />
          ~~~~~~~~

  src/Scratch.tsx:3:3 - 'town' is declared here.
    3   town: string
        ~~~~

src/Scratch.tsx:19:42 - error TS2322: Type '{ name: string; twon: string; }' is not assignable to type 'IntrinsicAttributes & SiteNameProps'.
  Property 'twon' does not exist on type 'IntrinsicAttributes & SiteNameProps'.

19       <SiteName name="Otter Creek Mouth" twon="Seal Rock" />
                                            ~~~~

src/Scratch.tsx:20:17 - error TS2322: Type 'number' is not assignable to type 'string'.

20       <SiteName name={42} town="Calder Bay" />
                   ~~~~

  src/Scratch.tsx:2:3 - The expected type comes from property 'name' which is declared here on type 'IntrinsicAttributes & SiteNameProps'
    2   name: string
        ~~~~


Found 3 errors in the same file, starting at: src/Scratch.tsx:18

Read each one in four moves: where (file:line:column), what (the code and the message), the line with ~~~~ under the problem, why it knows (the indented follow-up pointing at the interface). TS2741: town is missing. TS2322 "does not exist": twon is not a prop; IntrinsicAttributes & SiteNameProps means React's own props plus yours. TS2322 "the expected type comes from": name must be a string.

Glossary

TermMeaning
propsThe one object React passes to a component: every attribute, plus children
destructuringUnpacking an object in the parameter list: ({ name, town }: SiteNameProps)
IntrinsicAttributesReact's own props every element accepts (such as key), joined with yours in error messages

boolean shorthand<TideTime highlight /> means highlight={true}
childrenThe content between a component's tags, passed as a prop
ReactNodeThe type for anything React can draw: elements, text, numbers, arrays of nodes, null, undefined, booleans

indexed access typeSite['image']: the type of one property of a type

react(immutability)oxlint's warning when code changes a prop or a hook argument

Session 6: Lists, Conditional Rendering, State and Events

Your workbook for Session 6: the key ideas of Lessons 3.3 and 3.4, two worked examples, both guided labs, predict-then-check practice, the lesson projects' checklists, a self-check for each lesson and your exit ticket.

Lessons 3.3–3.4 Session 6 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Turn arrays into elements with map, with keys taken from the data, and filter or order first without changing the original.
  • Show parts of a component only when they apply, and avoid the 0 trap.
  • Write lookup tables keyed by a union so TypeScript keeps them complete; let a union on kind decide what to draw.
  • Give components state with useState (and a type argument when it needs one), handle events with typed handlers, update from snapshots and copies.
  • Place state in the closest common parent, pass it down with callback props, and derive everything else.

Lessons

3.3

Lists and Conditional Rendering

By the end you can
  • Turn an array into elements with map, filtering and ordering it first without changing the original
  • Explain what a key does, choose a stable one from the data, and read React's key warnings
  • Show parts of a component only when they apply with if and early returns, ? :, && and null, and avoid the 0 trap
  • Give every value of a union a label with a Record lookup table that TypeScript keeps complete
  • Let a discriminated union decide what a component draws, with TypeScript narrowing each case
  • Build the HQ's events page from a typed CleanupEvent[] with EventList, EventCard, StatusBadge and SpotsLine, including an empty state
Key ideas
  • map turns data into elements; filter, toSorted and toReversed shape the list first and return NEW arrays (sort and reverse change the original).
  • A key is a name tag: from the data (event.id), unique among siblings, stable, on the element map returns. Index keys silence the warning without fixing anything once rows have state.
  • Four conditional tools: if + early return (the whole component), ? : (one of two things), && with a REAL boolean (length > 0, never a bare number: 0 draws), null (draw nothing).
  • Record<Union, T> lookup tables are exhaustive: TS2741 for a missing key, TS2561 for a misspelled one; lookups are never undefined. Record<string, T> checks nothing and every lookup may be undefined (TS2532).
  • A discriminated union on kind lets a switch draw each case with that case's data (TypeScript narrows in each case). A switch with a missing case still compiles (Lesson 5.1 fixes that).
  • An empty list deserves a sentence, not a blank space.
Worked example: a table TypeScript keeps complete

StatusBadge's table, typed Record<EventStatus, { label: string; tone: BadgeTone }>, with the draft row deleted. npx tsc -b:

src/components/StatusBadge.tsx:5:7 - error TS2741: Property 'draft' is missing in type '{ scheduled: { label: string; tone: "info"; }; completed: { label: string; tone: "success"; }; cancelled: { label: string; tone: "danger"; }; }' but required in type 'Record<EventStatus, { label: string; tone: BadgeTone; }>'.

5 const statusBadges: Record<EventStatus, { label: string; tone: BadgeTone }> = {
        ~~~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/components/StatusBadge.tsx:5

The message spells out the type of each entry you DID write and names the one you did not. The same table can never fall behind the API: if EventStatus grows a fifth word one day, every table keyed by it stops compiling until it has the new entry. Reading from it is safe too: statusBadges[status] is never undefined, because a status can only be one of the four keys.

3.4

State and Events

By the end you can
  • Explain why a plain variable cannot remember anything between renders, and give a component state with useState
  • Handle clicks, typing and menu changes with typed event handlers such as ChangeEvent<HTMLInputElement>, and pass a function instead of calling it
  • Choose when useState<T> needs a type argument: unions, empty arrays and "nothing yet" values
  • Update state from a snapshot or with an updater function, and copy objects and arrays instead of changing them
  • Lift shared state to the closest common parent, pass it down as props with callback props for changes, and derive everything else
  • Add search, a status filter and per-card details to the HQ's events page, plus readable dates with a formatDate helper
Key ideas
  • Local variables last one render. const [value, setValue] = useState(initial) keeps a value between renders; calling the setter stores the new value and schedules a re-render. Hooks run at the top level, in the same order every render.
  • Event props take a function: onClick={() => setOpen(true)}, never onClick={setOpen(true)}. Inline handlers are typed by the prop; named ones take ChangeEvent<HTMLInputElement> (or HTMLSelectElement, MouseEvent<HTMLButtonElement>...).
  • useState needs a type argument for a union (useState<StatusFilter>('all')), an empty array (useState<string[]>([])) and nothing-yet (useState<CleanupEvent | null>(null)).
  • Each render sees a snapshot: use an updater when the new value depends on the old one (setCount((c) => c + 1)); make NEW arrays and objects ([...ids, id], filter, { ...filters, query }).
  • Lift state to the closest common parent; pass values down and changes up with callback props (a controlled component). Derive whatever can be computed: stored copies fall a keystroke behind.
  • A select hands over a string: narrow it with a type guard (isEventStatus) before passing it up, no as.
Worked example: an error far from its cause

Without the type argument (useState('all')), the status state is a string, and TypeScript reports it where the WIDER type is used, not where it was made:

src/pages/EventsPage.tsx:19:74 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'string' is not assignable to parameter of type 'StatusFilter'.

19   const visible = events.filter((event) => matchesFilters(event, search, status))
                                                                            ~~~~~~

src/pages/EventsPage.tsx:38:9 - error TS2322: Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'StatusFilter'.

38         status={status}
           ~~~~~~

  src/components/EventFilters.tsx:9:3 - The expected type comes from property 'status' which is declared here on type 'IntrinsicAttributes & EventFiltersProps'
    9   status: StatusFilter
        ~~~~~~


Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/pages/EventsPage.tsx:19

The mistake is on line 15; the errors are on lines 19 and 38. When an error points at a line that looks right, follow the type back to where it was made. With useState<StatusFilter>('all') both errors go away, and setStatus('finished') becomes an error instead.

Glossary

TermMeaning
keyReact's name tag for an item in a list: from the data, unique among siblings, stable
early returnif (...) return ... at the top of a component: draw something else and stop
the 0 trapcount && <p/> draws a 0 when count is 0; compare explicitly: count > 0 &&
lookup tableAn object with one entry per value of a union: Record<Terrain, string>
exhaustiveEvery possible value handled, checked by the compiler
discriminated unionA union whose members share a property (kind) with a different word in each
stateData React keeps for a component between renders (useState)
HookA function whose name starts with use, called at the top level of a component
setter, updaterThe function useState returns to change the state; a function passed to it: (current) => current + 1
snapshotThe state value for ONE render; the setter does not change it, it asks for a new render
ChangeEvent<T>React's type for a change event on element T (HTMLInputElement, HTMLSelectElement)
controlled componentA component that shows the value it is given and reports changes through callbacks
lifting state upMoving state to the closest common parent of everything that reads or changes it
derived valueWorked out from state and props during render, never stored

Session 7: Forms and Actions

Your workbook for Session 7: the key ideas of Lesson 3.5, a worked example of the three places that check, the guided lab, predict-then-check practice, the project's checklist, a self-check and your exit ticket.

Lesson 3.5 Session 7 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Choose controlled or uncontrolled inputs for a field, and explain the difference.
  • Pass a function to a form's action and read FormData safely: FormDataEntryValue | null narrowed with typeof, never as.
  • Turn each submit into typed state with useActionState, and use isPending while it runs.
  • Keep what people typed after a failed submit (defaultValue, the select's key), and write messages that say how to fix the problem, connected with aria-invalid and aria-describedby.
  • Build the HQ's Plan a cleanup form with checks that agree with the API.

Lesson

3.5

Forms and Actions

By the end you can
  • Explain the difference between controlled and uncontrolled inputs, and choose the right one for a field
  • Pass a function to a form's action and read the submitted FormData safely, narrowing FormDataEntryValue | null without as
  • Use useActionState to turn each submit into typed state, and isPending to show that a submit is in progress
  • Keep what people typed when a submit fails, and know why React resets a form after an action
  • Write field messages that say how to fix the problem, and connect them to their fields with aria-invalid and aria-describedby
  • Build the HQ's "Plan a cleanup" form, with checks that agree with the API's own rules
Key ideas
  • Controlled inputs keep their value in React state (value + onChange): for values the screen needs while typing. Uncontrolled inputs keep it in the browser (name, defaultValue) and are read through FormData when the form is sent.
  • A function in a form's action receives the FormData; React prevents the page load and RESETS the form afterwards (fields go back to their defaultValue).
  • formData.get() returns FormDataEntryValue | null (string, File or null): narrow with typeof value === 'string'. Every field needs a name; the submitting button's name and value are sent too.
  • useActionState(action, initialState) returns [state, formAction, isPending]; the action gets the previous state first, then the FormData, and returns the new state (a union on kind works well).
  • Messages that help say how to fix the problem, sit under their field, and are linked with aria-invalid and aria-describedby. noValidate keeps every message in one place. The API checks everything again.
  • The select quirk (React 19.3): a select reads its defaultValue only when it is created; key={values.siteId} makes a new one after the reset.
Worked example: three places that check

The project's mistakes ("Dune", North Jetty, 09:00 to 08:00, 12 volunteers, no date, no meeting point) checked by the form's own validateEvent (run with Node in the finished app):

{
  "title": [
    "Use at least 5 characters for the title."
  ],
  "date": [
    "Pick a date."
  ],
  "endTime": [
    "The end time must be after the start time."
  ],
  "meetingPoint": [
    "Say where volunteers meet (at least 3 characters)."
  ]
}

and by shoreline-api, which answers a POST from an organizer with status 400:

HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request
content-type: application/json; charset=utf-8

{
  "error": "validation_failed",
  "message": "Some fields need attention.",
  "fieldErrors": {
    "title": [
      "Use at least 5 characters for the title."
    ],
    "date": [
      "Enter a date as YYYY-MM-DD."
    ],
    "endTime": [
      "The end time must be after the start time."
    ],
    "meetingPoint": [
      "Say where volunteers meet (at least 3 characters)."
    ]
  }
}

The same four fields in the same shape (field name: a list of messages), so in Lesson 7.2 the API's answer can be shown with the same FieldError component. Three messages are word for word the same; the empty date is reworded for people, who use a date picker. The API checks everything again because anyone can send it anything: it is the authority.

P2
Predict what npx tsc -b says, then put it in src/Scratch.tsx and run it. (useActionState with one argument.)
import { useActionState } from 'react'

function addBags(previous: number, formData: FormData): number {
  const bags = formData.get('bags')
  return typeof bags === 'string' ? previous + Number(bags) : previous
}

export function BagCount() {
  const [total, formAction] = useActionState(addBags)
  return (
    <form action={formAction}>
      <input aria-label="Bags" name="bags" type="number" />
      <button type="submit">Add</button>
      <p>{total} bags so far</p>
    </form>
  )
}

Glossary

TermMeaning
controlled inputReact state holds the value (value + onChange)
uncontrolled inputThe browser holds the value; you read it from FormData (name, defaultValue)
defaultValueThe starting text of an uncontrolled field (what the reset goes back to)
form actionA function in <form action={...}>: React calls it with the FormData and resets the form after it
FormData, FormDataEntryValueThe browser's collection of named fields; one value is File | string
submitterThe button that submitted the form; its name and value are sent too
useActionStateTurns each submit into state: [state, formAction, isPending]
isPendingTrue from the submit until the action's promise settles
noValidateTurns off the browser's own check bubbles so your messages are the only ones
aria-invalid, aria-describedbyTell assistive technology that a field is wrong and where its message is
fieldErrorsThe API's error shape: field name to a list of messages
SubmitEventReact's type for submit events in an onSubmit handler (FormEvent is deprecated)

Session 8: Styling and Accessible Components

Your workbook for Session 8: Lesson 3.6's key ideas, a worked example, the guided lab, predict-then-check practice, the self-check and the journal, then the Part 1 project kickoff (Juniper Hill Community Garden): setup, the TODO A worksheet, milestones and the rubric.

Lesson 3.6 Session 8 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Move a component's styles into its own CSS Module and read what TypeScript knows about it (string | undefined).
  • Pick a variant with a union-typed prop (styles[tone]) and type an inline style with CSSProperties.
  • Give every focusable element a visible ring with one token, and keep tokens and page-wide rules in index.css.
  • Check components the way real teams do: jsx-a11y, the browser's audits, a keyboard pass, and names that make sense out of context.
  • Start the Part 1 project: Juniper Hill Community Garden's Plot Board, set up and typed.

Lesson

3.6

Styling and Accessible Components

By the end you can
  • Explain why one global stylesheet stops scaling, and how CSS Modules scope class names to a component
  • Import a CSS Module in a typed component, combine classes, pick a variant from a union-typed prop, and read the string | undefined type TypeScript gives each class
  • Use inline style objects typed as CSSProperties for values that come from data, and know when not to
  • Self-host a font, keep design tokens global, and give every focusable element a visible focus ring
  • Check components for accessibility with the linter's jsx-a11y rules, the browser's audits, and a keyboard pass, and fix what only a person notices
Key ideas
  • CSS Modules: a file named Name.module.css; import styles from './Name.module.css' gives an object from your class names to unique generated ones (_note_1pozh_1). No clashes, no leftovers.
  • What TypeScript knows: every module is CSSModuleClasses (any string key); with noUncheckedIndexedAccess each class is string | undefined. A misspelled class compiles: look at the page (or write a declaration file, Exercise 3).
  • Names and variants: camelCase classes (.skipLink); combine with a template literal; pick a variant with a union-typed prop: styles[tone]. Sharing is importing another module (cardStyles).
  • Inline styles: an object typed CSSProperties (camelCase, numbers become px), only for values that come from data.
  • Tokens stay global: colors, the base font and the :focus-visible ring in index.css; a module can SET a token for everything inside it (--focus in the header). Fonts self-hosted with Fontsource.
  • Accessible components: the right element first, landmarks and headings, a name for every control that makes sense out of context, alt that describes or alt="", a visible focus ring, contrast and not color alone, ARIA only for state. Check with jsx-a11y, the browser's audits and your own keyboard.
Worked example: what TypeScript knows about a CSS Module

A helper that promises a string meets a CSS Module:

import styles from './Scratch.module.css'

// A helper that promises to return a string
function noteClass(): string {
  return styles.note
}

export function TideNote() {
  return <aside className={noteClass()}>High tide at 11:40</aside>
}

npx tsc -b:

src/Scratch.tsx:5:3 - error TS2322: Type 'string | undefined' is not assignable to type 'string'.
  Type 'undefined' is not assignable to type 'string'.

5   return styles.note
    ~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:5

Read it the Lesson 1.1 way: where (src/Scratch.tsx:5:3), what (TS2322: string | undefined into string), why: vite/client types every module as "any string key to a string", and noUncheckedIndexedAccess adds undefined to every read. In JSX, className accepts string | undefined, so className={styles.note} is fine.

And the other side: a misspelled class compiles. className={styles.ntoe} is undefined, React drops the attribute, and both tools stay silent. With npm run lint after npx tsc -b:

> shoreline-hq@0.0.0 lint
> oxlint

Found 0 warnings and 0 errors.
Finished in …ms on 31 files with 150 rules using … threads.

Glossary

TermMeaning
CSS ModuleA Name.module.css file whose class names are renamed to be unique to that file
CSSModuleClassesvite/client's type for a module: { readonly [key: string]: string } (any key)
index signatureA type for "any key of this kind"; with noUncheckedIndexedAccess each read may be undefined
design tokenA named value in :root (--link, --focus) that every component uses; inherited, so a module can set it for its subtree
side-effect importimport '@fontsource-variable/outfit': runs a file for what it does, not for what it exports
CSSPropertiesReact's type for a style object: camelCase properties, numbers become pixels
accessibility (a11y)Whether people who use a keyboard, a screen reader, zoom or voice control can use the app
accessible nameWhat assistive technology calls a control (a button's text, a field's label)
:focus-visibleMatches when the browser judges a focus ring helpful: keyboard focus, text fields
visually hidden textText in the page for screen readers, clipped to a 1-pixel box so it takes no room
jsx-a11yoxlint's plugin with the accessibility rules for HTML elements and ARIA in TSX
WCAGThe Web Content Accessibility Guidelines: contrast 4.5:1 for text, 3:1 for focus rings and borders

Session 10: Effects and Fetching

Your workbook for Session 10: the Starting Part 2 checklist and a Part 1 refresher, then Lesson 4.1's key ideas, a worked example, the guided lab, predict-then-check practice, a self-check and your exit ticket.

Lesson 4.1 Session 10 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Start Part 2 with a ready machine: the environment check ends with READY on your own Part 1 code or on the catch-up code.
  • Explain what a side effect is, when an effect runs (the dependency array) and why Strict Mode runs it twice in development.
  • Fetch typed data in an effect and read Promise<T>, EffectCallback and AbortSignal in TypeScript's messages.
  • Show loading, failed and ready states with a generic LoadState<T>, and messages people can act on.
  • Cancel a request nobody waits for with an AbortController, and practice slow and failing answers with the API's switches.

Lesson

4.1

Effects and Fetching

By the end you can
  • Explain what a side effect is, why it does not belong in rendering, and when you do not need an effect at all
  • Write useEffect with the right dependency array and a cleanup function, and explain why Strict Mode runs effects twice in development
  • Fetch typed data in an effect, reading Promise<T>, Response and AbortSignal in TypeScript's messages
  • Model loading, error and ready states with a generic discriminated union, and turn API errors into messages people understand
  • Cancel requests with AbortController, and practice slow and failing answers with the API's --latency and ?_status switches
Key ideas
  • Side effect: anything a function does besides returning its result (a request, a timer, the tab title, a window listener). Rendering stays pure; effects run after the screen is updated.
  • useEffect(effect, deps): the effect may return a cleanup (void | Destructor); React runs the cleanup before the next run and when the component goes away. [] = once, [x] = when x changed, no array = after every render (almost never).
  • Strict Mode (development only) starts, cleans up and starts every effect again to prove the cleanup works. Fix the cleanup, never remove StrictMode.
  • Fetching: getSites(): Promise<Site[]>; .then knows the type. useEffect(async …) is TS2345: start the async work inside the effect. fetch resolves on a 500 (ok false) and rejects only with no answer.
  • States: LoadState<T> = loading | failed (message) | ready (data); empty is ready with zero items. ApiError (the API answered with an error status) vs TypeError (no answer): two messages. Annotate .catch((error: unknown) => …).
  • Canceling: an AbortController per run; its signal goes to fetch; abort in the cleanup; ignore an aborted request in the catch. The latest run wins.
Worked example: the sites page's effect

The sites page loads its sites in an effect. This is the finished Lesson 4.1 code (Step 3):

shoreline-hq/src/pages/SitesPage.tsx
export function SitesPage() {
  const [state, setState] = useState<LoadState<Site[]>>({ kind: 'loading' })

  // After the first render: ask the API for the sites, and cancel the request if the page goes away
  useEffect(() => {
    const controller = new AbortController()
    getSites(controller.signal)
      .then((sites) => setState({ kind: 'ready', data: sites }))
      .catch((error: unknown) => {
        if (controller.signal.aborted) return
        console.error(error)
        setState({ kind: 'failed', message: describeError(error) })
      })
    return () => controller.abort()
  }, [])

Read it in four moves. Start in the right state: useState<LoadState<Site[]>>({ kind: 'loading' }), so a slow screen never looks empty. Ask after the first render: the array is [], so the effect runs once (twice on the dev server: Strict Mode). Two endings: .then makes it ready with the data; .catch ignores a request the cleanup canceled, logs the details for you and shows a message for people (describeError). Sign off: the cleanup aborts the request if the page goes away first.

And the async version TypeScript refuses, with its reason:

npx tsc -b
src/Scratch.tsx:8:13 - error TS2345: Argument of type '() => Promise<void>' is not assignable to parameter of type 'EffectCallback'.
Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:8

Glossary

TermMeaning
side effectAnything a function does besides returning its result: a request, a timer, the tab title, a listener
useEffectRuns code after React has updated the screen, to keep something outside React in step
cleanup functionWhat an effect returns: undoes the effect before the next run and when the component goes away
dependency arrayThe values from the component the effect reads; a change runs the effect again
exhaustive-depsoxlint's rule that lists the dependencies an effect forgot
Strict ModeDevelopment-only checks: effects start, clean up and start again
Promise<T>An answer that is not here yet, typed by what it will hold
EffectCallback / DestructorReact's types: an effect returns void or a cleanup function
response.oktrue for statuses 200 to 299; fetch resolves on any answer, rejects only with none
LoadState<T>loading | failed (message) | ready (data): a generic discriminated union on kind
ApiErrorThe HQ's error class for an answer with an error status (status, code, fieldErrors)
AbortController / AbortSignalThe browser's way to cancel a fetch: pass the signal, call abort()
race conditionAn older, slower answer arriving last and overwriting the newer one
X-Total-CountThe response header with the total number of items in a paged list
role="alert"A message screen readers announce at once (LoadStatus's failure)
catch-up codelesson-13-start: the course's code at the end of Part 1, for anyone starting Part 2

Lessons

4.2

Custom Hooks

By the end you can
  • Explain what a custom Hook is, when a plain function is the better choice, and why two components that call the same Hook do not share its state
  • Follow the Rules of Hooks inside your own Hooks, and read the linter's rules-of-hooks errors
  • Type what a Hook returns: nothing, one value, a tuple with as const, or an object with an interface
  • Write generic Hooks (useDebouncedValue<T>, useApi<T>) whose types follow the arguments you pass
  • Pass a function into a Hook safely with useEffectEvent, and explain what a data library like TanStack Query adds on top of a Hook like useApi
Key ideas
  • Custom Hook: a function whose name starts with use and calls Hooks. It shares logic between components, never state: two callers, two states, two requests.
  • Rules of Hooks inside your Hook: top level only, never after an early return; only from components and Hooks. The linter finds Hooks by name (rules-of-hooks is an error; tsc is silent).
  • Return shapes: nothing (void), one value, a tuple (as const, or every name gets the union), an object (an exported interface in the return annotation).
  • Generic Hooks take T from the arguments: useDebouncedValue(query, 300) is a string; useApi('/sites', getSites) is ApiResult<Site[]>.
  • Key and load: a function made during render is new every render; in a dependency array it floods the API. The key decides when; useEffectEvent reads the latest load without triggering.
  • What useApi lacks (cache, retries, refetch on focus, invalidation) is why TanStack Query replaces it in Lesson 7.1.
Worked example: useApi, read in four moves

The Hook every data page uses after this lesson (the finished Lesson 4.2 code):

shoreline-hq/src/hooks/useApi.ts
export function useApi<T>(key: string, load: (signal: AbortSignal) => Promise<T>): ApiResult<T> {
  const [state, setState] = useState<LoadState<T>>({ kind: 'loading' })
  const [attempt, setAttempt] = useState(0)
  // The latest load function, without making the effect run again when it changes
  const loadLatest = useEffectEvent(load)

  useEffect(() => {
    const controller = new AbortController()
    loadLatest(controller.signal)
      .then((data) => setState({ kind: 'ready', data }))
      .catch((error: unknown) => {
        if (controller.signal.aborted) return
        console.error(error)
        setState({ kind: 'failed', message: describeError(error) })
      })
    return () => controller.abort()
  }, [key, attempt])

  function reload() {
    setState({ kind: 'loading' })
    setAttempt((count) => count + 1)
  }

  return { state, reload }
}

Read it in four moves. Same pattern as 4.1: LoadState, AbortController, the aborted check, describeError. Key = WHAT: a string; the same key on every render means "nothing changed", so it is in the array (with attempt). Load = HOW: a new function on every render is fine, because the effect calls loadLatest, the useEffectEvent copy that always sees the latest load without triggering anything. Try again for free: reload shows loading and counts one more attempt in the same click.

A page then needs one line: const sites = useApi('/sites', getSites); TypeScript infers ApiResult<Site[]> from getSites.

  • Task 3: useLoadedCount: read how many requests the starter sends for one key; make it one.

P1
The toggle Hook now ends with return [on, toggle] as const. What does npx tsc -b print?
import { useState } from 'react'

function useToggle(initial: boolean) {
  const [on, setOn] = useState(initial)
  const toggle = () => setOn((current) => !current)
  return [on, toggle] as const
}

export function DetailsButton() {
  const [open, toggle] = useToggle(false)
  return (
    <button type="button" aria-expanded={open} onClick={toggle}>
      Details
    </button>
  )
}
P2
A Hook returns early when the title is empty. What does npm run lint say?
import { useEffect } from 'react'

export function useTabTitle(title: string) {
  if (title === '') return
  useEffect(() => {
    document.title = title
  }, [title])
}

4.3

Refs and the DOM

By the end you can
  • Explain what a ref is, when to choose a ref over state, and why reading a ref while rendering is a mistake
  • Type refs to values and to DOM elements (useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null)), and read the errors TypeScript gives for null, the wrong element and React 19's required argument
  • Use callback refs, including React 19's cleanup function, and avoid the arrow-function return that React 19's types reject
  • Pass a ref to your own component as an ordinary prop, the React 19 way
  • Manage focus on purpose: a keyboard shortcut, the first invalid field, and a modal dialog rendered through a portal that gives the focus back when it closes
Key ideas
  • A ref is an object with current that survives renders; changing it renders nothing. If the screen should change when it changes, it is state; otherwise it can be a ref. Never read ref.current during render.
  • DOM refs: useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null) → current is HTMLInputElement | null; use ?.. The type argument names the element (a div's ref on an input is TS2322).
  • Callback refs: ref={(el) => { … }} runs when the element arrives; in React 19 it may return a cleanup, which must return nothing (braces!).
  • Ref as a prop (React 19): ref?: Ref<HTMLInputElement> in a component's props; the component chooses which element a parent reaches. forwardRef is the old way.
  • Focus management: an element disappears → somewhere useful; a form finds problems → the first field to fix; a dialog opens → inside it; closes → back to its opener. Document listeners live in an effect (the browser's KeyboardEvent).
  • Portals and <dialog>: createPortal renders into body while staying in React's tree; showModal() gives the top layer, an inert page, Escape and initial focus.
Worked example: the HQ's Dialog

The dialog every confirmation in the HQ uses (the finished Lesson 4.3 code):

shoreline-hq/src/components/Dialog.tsx
export function Dialog({ title, onClose, children }: DialogProps) {
  const dialogRef = useRef<HTMLDialogElement>(null)
  const openerRef = useRef<Element | null>(null)
  const titleId = useId()

  useEffect(() => {
    const dialog = dialogRef.current
    if (dialog === null) return
    if (!dialog.open) {
      openerRef.current = document.activeElement // remember where the focus was
      dialog.showModal() // focus moves into the dialog, the page behind it goes inert
    }
    return () => {
      // Closing: give the focus back to the control that opened the dialog
      const opener = openerRef.current
      if (opener instanceof HTMLElement) opener.focus()
    }
  }, [])

  return createPortal(
    <dialog ref={dialogRef} aria-labelledby={titleId} className={styles.dialog} onClose={onClose}>
      <h2 id={titleId}>{title}</h2>
      {children}
    </dialog>,
    document.body,

Two refs, two jobs: dialogRef holds the element so the effect can call showModal(); openerRef holds a plain value nobody sees (where the focus was), so the cleanup can give the focus back. The !dialog.open check keeps Strict Mode's second run from remembering the wrong opener. createPortal puts the <dialog> in body, while it stays inside EventCard in React's tree (its props, state and events).

Lessons

4.4

Suspense, use() and Error Boundaries

By the end you can
  • Explain what Suspense does and where a <Suspense> boundary belongs in a component tree
  • Read a promise while rendering with use(), typed, and keep the promise stable so it is not requested again on every render
  • Write a typed error boundary as a class component, and say which errors it catches and which it does not
  • Design fallbacks that help people: a clear message, "not found" told apart from "could not load", and a Try again that really tries again
  • Judge where Suspense belongs in a single-page app today, and what React Router (Module 6) and TanStack Query (Module 7) take over later
Key ideas
  • Suspense: a component that reads data that is not there yet suspends; the nearest <Suspense> shows its fallback; everything outside stays.
  • use(promise) returns the resolved value (use<T>(usable: Usable<T>): T). The promise must be the same on every render: a cache. Never in try/catch.
  • Error boundaries catch errors thrown while rendering (a rejected use() too), not handlers or async code. A class: typed union state, static getDerivedStateFromError, a retry arrow.
  • Fallbacks that help: not found is not retried; could not load gets Try again, which forgets the failed promise first.
Worked example: the cleanup page
shoreline-hq/src/pages/EventPage.tsx
export function EventPage({ eventId }: EventPageProps) {
  return (
    <>
      <p>
        <a href="/events">← All cleanups</a>
      </p>
      <ErrorBoundary fallback={EventProblem} onRetry={() => forgetEvent(eventId)}>
        <Suspense fallback={<LoadStatus state={{ kind: 'loading' }} what="the cleanup" />}>
          <EventDetails eventId={eventId} />
        </Suspense>
      </ErrorBoundary>
    </>
  )
}

Read it from the outside in: the back link is outside both boundaries (always there); the ErrorBoundary's fallback is EventProblem and its onRetry forgets the failed promise; the Suspense fallback is the familiar LoadStatus box; EventDetails inside only handles the happy path.

5.1

useReducer with Discriminated Unions

By the end you can
  • Recognize state whose updates follow rules, and explain why useReducer suits it better than several useState calls
  • Describe every state change as an action, typed as a discriminated union, and read the errors TypeScript gives for a wrong action
  • Write a pure reducer that returns new state instead of changing the old one, and type useReducer and dispatch by letting TypeScript infer them
  • Use the never type and an assertNever helper so the compiler reports every switch that misses a case
  • Build a check-in desk with undo for today's cleanup, and say which parts of it later lessons keep
Key ideas
  • Reducer: (state, action) => newState; pure, never changes what it got, no side effects, the same state when nothing changed.
  • Actions are a discriminated union on type; a wrong action is a compile error.
  • useReducer infers its types from the annotated reducer; useReducer<S, A> is TS2344 in React 19. dispatch is stable; Strict Mode runs reducers twice.
  • never / assertNever: the default that only accepts nothing: tsc names any member no case handles. default: return state swallows it.
  • useState or useReducer: several values that change together by rules → a reducer; server data → neither.
Worked example: rosterReducer
shoreline-hq/src/reducers/rosterReducer.ts
export function rosterReducer(state: RosterState, action: RosterAction): RosterState {
  switch (action.type) {
    case 'checkIn':
      return setMark(state, action.signupId, 'attended')
    case 'markNoShow':
      return setMark(state, action.signupId, 'no-show')
    case 'clear':
      return setMark(state, action.signupId, undefined)
    case 'undo': {
      const last = state.history.at(-1)
      if (last === undefined) {
        return state
      }
      return {
        marks: withMark(state.marks, last.signupId, last.before),
        history: state.history.slice(0, -1),
      }
    }
    default:
      return assertNever(action)
  }
}

One line per action, plus undo: the newest history entry, its before mark put back, the entry dropped. The default is the exhaustive check.

import { initialRoster, rosterReducer, type RosterAction } from './src/reducers/rosterReducer.ts'

const actions: RosterAction[] = [
  { type: 'checkIn', signupId: 's088' },
  { type: 'checkIn', signupId: 's088' },
  { type: 'markNoShow', signupId: 's088' },
  { type: 'undo' },
  { type: 'undo' },
  { type: 'undo' },
]

let state = initialRoster
for (const action of actions) {
  const next = rosterReducer(state, action)
  console.log(action.type, next.marks.get('s088') ?? 'expected', next.history.length, next === state)
  state = next
}
P3
A reducer ends with default: return state and the union has a removed member with no case. What does npx tsc -b print?
import { useReducer } from 'react'

interface Tally {
  bags: number
  weightKg: number
}

type TallyAction =
  | { type: 'added'; weightKg: number }
  | { type: 'removed'; weightKg: number }
  | { type: 'reset' }

function tallyReducer(tally: Tally, action: TallyAction): Tally {
  switch (action.type) {
    case 'added':
      return { bags: tally.bags + 1, weightKg: tally.weightKg + action.weightKg }
    case 'reset':
      return { bags: 0, weightKg: 0 }
    default:
      return tally
  }
}

export function BagTally() {
  const [tally, dispatch] = useReducer(tallyReducer, { bags: 0, weightKg: 0 })

  return (
    <section>
      <p role="status">
        {tally.bags} bags, {tally.weightKg} kg
      </p>
      <button type="button" onClick={() => dispatch({ type: 'added', weightKg: 4 })}>
        Add a 4 kg bag
      </button>
      <button type="button" onClick={() => dispatch({ type: 'reset' })}>
        Reset
      </button>
    </section>
  )
}

Lesson

5.2

Typed Context

By the end you can
  • Explain prop drilling, and decide when a value belongs in context and when a plain prop is clearer
  • Create a typed context with createContext, provide it with React 19's <Context value>, and read it with useContext
  • Avoid the "undefined context" trap: type the context as T | undefined and write a useAuth() Hook that fails loudly outside its provider and never returns undefined
  • Sign in against a real API with roles, keep the session in sessionStorage, and send the token with every request
  • Make data depend on who is asking: drafts, organizer-only buttons and a live roster that load again when someone signs in or out
Key ideas
  • Prop drilling = passing a value through components that only hand it on. Context sends one value to every component below a provider, wherever it sits: for values many distant components need and that change rarely. A direct child still gets a prop.
  • Three parts: createContext<T>(default); <SomeContext value={…}> (React 19; .Provider is the older spelling); useContext(SomeContext) reads the NEAREST provider above, or the default when there is none. When the value changes, every reader renders again.
  • The undefined trap: a fake default compiles and fails silently. When no default makes sense, type the context T | undefined with undefined as the default, and read it only through a Hook (useAuth()) that throws outside the provider and returns T.
  • Two files: the context, its type and its Hook in a .ts; the provider component in a .tsx (Fast Refresh and oxlint's only-export-components).
  • The session: sign-in = POST /api/auth/sign-in → a token and the user (role organizer or volunteer); sessionStorage (survives reloads, one tab), checked like API data on the way in, written only by the provider, sent as Authorization: Bearer … with every request.
  • Who asks is part of the question: the same URL answers 15 cleanups to a guest and 17 to an organizer, so useApi and caches key their data by the viewer. Hiding a button is convenience; the API decides (403).
Worked example: the type that tells the truth

The HQ's context says the truth: outside a provider there is no sign-in, so its type is AuthValue | undefined. A component in src/Scratch.tsx that reads it directly:

import { useContext } from 'react'
import { AuthContext } from './auth/AuthContext.ts'

export function OrganizerNote() {
  const { isOrganizer } = useContext(AuthContext)
  return isOrganizer ? <p>Drafts are listed below.</p> : null
}
npx tsc -b
src/Scratch.tsx:5:11 - error TS2339: Property 'isOrganizer' does not exist on type 'AuthValue | undefined'.

5   const { isOrganizer } = useContext(AuthContext)
            ~~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:5

"Does not exist on type AuthValue | undefined" means: this might be undefined, and undefined has no isOrganizer. Instead of ?. in every component, the project checks once, in a Hook:

shoreline-hq/src/auth/AuthContext.ts
// undefined is the value outside an AuthProvider: a mistake that useAuth() reports at once
export const AuthContext = createContext<AuthValue | undefined>(undefined)

/** The sign-in state. Only works inside <AuthProvider>. */
export function useAuth(): AuthValue {
  const auth = useContext(AuthContext)
  if (auth === undefined) {
    throw new Error('useAuth() must be used inside <AuthProvider>')
  }
  return auth
}

After the if, auth is an AuthValue, so every caller of useAuth() gets one. A missing provider is now a blank page with a console message that says exactly what to fix, instead of a sign-in button that silently does nothing.

Glossary

TermMeaning
prop drillingPassing a value down through components that do not use it, only to reach one that does
contextA value React sends to every component below a provider, whatever sits in between
createContext<T>(default)Makes a context object; the default is what a component gets with no provider above it
provider<SomeContext value={…}> (React 19): gives the value to the part of the tree inside it
useContextReads the value of the NEAREST provider above the component
the undefined trapA fake default that compiles and turns a missing provider into a silent bug
useAuth()The HQ's custom Hook: reads AuthContext, throws outside AuthProvider, returns AuthValue
Fast RefreshVite's update-in-place on save; keeps state only for files that export components only
session, tokenWho is signed in (the API's answer) / the opaque string that proves it on every request
Authorization: Bearer …The request header that carries the token
sessionStorageBrowser storage that survives reloads, belongs to one tab and ends when the tab closes
401 / 403Not signed in (or the sign-in ended) / signed in, but not allowed
RequestInitThe type of fetch's options object (method, headers, body, signal…)
weak typeA type whose properties are all optional; TypeScript wants at least one property in common
