Lessons

5.3

Rendering Performance

By the end you can
  • Name the three reasons a component renders, and explain why rendering is not the same as changing the page
  • Measure renders with the React DevTools Profiler and React's <Profiler> component before you change anything
  • Use memo, useMemo and useCallback with their TypeScript types, and recognize when they only add noise
  • Keep a context value from rendering every reader again when nothing in it changed
  • Turn on the React Compiler in a Vite 8 project, check what it optimized, and decide what you still write by hand
Key ideas
  • Three reasons to render: its own state changed, its parent rendered, a context it reads changed. Rendering = React calls the function; commit = React changes only the DOM that differs.
  • Measure first: the React DevTools Profiler (commits, flame graph, Ranked, "why did this render?") and <Profiler id onRender> with ProfilerOnRenderCallback, which only reports in development.
  • memo skips a component whose props are all the same (Object.is); a new function, object or children each render defeats it. useCallback keeps a function, useMemo a value, until a dependency changes: they work as a pair with memo.
  • Context: memo never blocks the third reason. Keep a provider's value the same object (useMemo + useCallback) when the provider renders for other reasons.
  • The React Compiler memoizes values, functions and JSX at build time (a Babel preset through @rolldown/plugin-babel in Vite 8). It skips components that break the Rules of React: zero lint warnings matter.
  • Still yours: pure components, low state and stable keys, a useMemo you measured as needed, fewer requests. The compiler is an assistant, not a guarantee.
Worked example: the price and the payoff of the compiler

Before the compiler, npm run build on the HQ ends like this (times and hashes differ on your machine):

dist/assets/index-6OdvjMRn.js                            252.23 kB │ gzip: 78.51 kB
✓ built in …ms

After Step 3's install and this vite.config.ts:

shoreline-hq/vite.config.ts
import react, { reactCompilerPreset } from '@vitejs/plugin-react'
import babel from '@rolldown/plugin-babel'
import { defineConfig } from 'vite'

// https://vite.dev/config/
export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [
    react(),
    // The React Compiler: works out what each component can reuse between renders
    babel({ presets: [reactCompilerPreset()] }),
  ],
})

the same build ends:

dist/assets/index-B_WIRHHo.js                            270.77 kB │ gzip: 85.10 kB
✓ built in …ms

The price: 2 more modules and 18.5 kB more JavaScript (6.6 kB gzipped). What it buys, measured by the lesson (render counts are the same on the dev server and the build):

5.4

Component Patterns

By the end you can
  • Build compound components that share state through a private, typed context, and explain when they beat one big configuration prop
  • Make tabs that follow the accessible tabs pattern: roles, aria-selected, one tab stop and arrow keys
  • Write a generic component with a render prop, and let TypeScript infer its type parameter from the props
  • Type a polymorphic component whose props follow its as prop, with ElementType and ComponentPropsWithRef
  • Choose between plain props, children, compound components, render props and an as prop for a new component
Key ideas
  • Compound components split one component into parts written as plain JSX; the parent shares its state through a private context the parts read (TabsValue | undefined + a throwing useTabs).
  • Accessible tabs: role="tablist" with a label, role="tab" + aria-selected, aria-controls / aria-labelledby, one tab stop (tabIndex 0 / -1), arrow keys, Home, End; panels hidden, not removed, so they keep their state.
  • Render props: a prop that is a function returning something to render (renderItem). Generic components (DataList<T>) infer T from their props and check the callbacks against it.
  • Constraints ask for exactly what the component uses: getKey beats requiring an id.
  • Polymorphic components: C extends ElementType = 'button', ComponentPropsWithRef<C>, Omit of the props the component owns: the props follow the as prop. Read long errors from the last line up.
  • Choosing: plain props → children → compound → render prop → custom Hook → as. Move down only when the one above gets in the way.
Worked example: props that follow as

One Button, typed so that its props follow its as prop:

shoreline-hq/src/components/Button.tsx
import type { ComponentPropsWithRef, ElementType } from 'react'
import styles from './Button.module.css'

export type ButtonVariant = 'primary' | 'secondary' | 'link'

/** Button's own props, plus every prop of the element or component named in `as` */
type ButtonProps<C extends ElementType> = {
  /** What to render: 'button' (the default), 'a', or a component such as a router link */
  as?: C
  variant?: ButtonVariant
} & Omit<ComponentPropsWithRef<C>, 'as' | 'className'>

// The HQ's one button look, on whatever element the job needs
export function Button<C extends ElementType = 'button'>({
  as,
  variant = 'secondary',
  ...props
}: ButtonProps<C>) {
  const Component = as ?? 'button'
  // A <button> in a form submits unless told otherwise: make "button" the default type
  const type = Component === 'button' ? 'button' : undefined
  return <Component type={type} {...props} className={styles[variant]} />
}

Three wrong uses in a scratch file, and the first line of each error npx tsc -b prints:

<p>
  <Button href="/events/new">Plan a cleanup</Button>
  <Button as="a" href="/events/new" disabled>
    Plan a cleanup
  </Button>
  <Button variant="danger">Cancel cleanup</Button>
</p>
src/Scratch.tsx:6:15 - error TS2322: Type '{ href: string; children: string; }' is not assignable to type 'IntrinsicAttributes & { as?: "button" | undefined; variant?: ButtonVariant | undefined; } & Omit<DetailedHTMLProps<ButtonHTMLAttributes<HTMLButtonElement>, HTMLButtonElement>, "as" | "className">'.
src/Scratch.tsx:7:41 - error TS2322: Type '{ as: "a"; href: string; disabled: true; children: string; }' is not assignable to type 'IntrinsicAttributes & { as?: "a" | undefined; variant?: ButtonVariant | undefined; } & Omit<DetailedHTMLProps<AnchorHTMLAttributes<HTMLAnchorElement>, HTMLAnchorElement>, "as" | "className">'.

src/Scratch.tsx
import type { ReactNode } from 'react'
import type { DebrisCount } from './api/types.ts'

interface IdListProps<T extends { id: string }> {
  items: T[]
  renderItem: (item: T) => ReactNode
}

// Only for items that have an id: the constraint lets the list use item.id as the key
function IdList<T extends { id: string }>({ items, renderItem }: IdListProps<T>) {
  return (
    <ul>
      {items.map((item) => (
        <li key={item.id}>{renderItem(item)}</li>
      ))}
    </ul>
  )
}

export function PiecesCounted({ items }: { items: DebrisCount[] }) {
  return <IdList items={items} renderItem={(item) => `${item.category}: ${item.count}`} />
}

Glossary

TermMeaning
render / commitReact calling a component to see what it returns / React changing the DOM where the result differs
memoizationRemembering a result and reusing it until its inputs change
memoWraps a component: React skips it when every prop is the same (Object.is) as last time
useCallback / useMemoKeep the same function / the same calculated value between renders until a dependency changes
Object.is, referential equalitySame string, number or boolean value; for objects, arrays and functions: the same object, not a look-alike
stale valueA function that keeps a value from an older render because a dependency was left out
React DevTools ProfilerThe browser extension's tab that records commits, a flame graph and why each component rendered
<Profiler>, ProfilerOnRenderCallbackReact's component for measuring from code, and its callback's type (development builds only)
React CompilerA build step that memoizes components and Hooks for you; it skips code that breaks the Rules of React
'use no memo'A directive that turns the compiler off for one function
compound componentParts that only work together (Tabs, TabList, Tab, TabPanel) sharing state through a private context
render propA prop whose value is a function that returns something to render (renderItem)
generic componentA component with a type parameter that TypeScript infers from its props (DataList<T>)
polymorphic component, asA component that renders the element or component named in its as prop, with that element's props
ElementType, ComponentPropsWithRef<C>Anything you can render as a tag / the props (ref included) of that tag or component
roving tabindexOnly the selected tab has tabIndex 0, so Tab enters the tab list once; arrow keys move inside it

Lessons

6.1

React Router 8

By the end you can
  • Explain what client-side routing changes compared with following plain links, and why the server must still answer every address
  • Describe an app's pages as typed route objects with createBrowserRouter, including a layout route, an index route and a catch-all * route
  • Navigate with Link and NavLink, style the current link through aria-current, and pass Link to a polymorphic component
  • Read URL parameters with useParams, narrow their string | undefined type, and reset a component's state with a key when only the parameter changes
  • Choose between a route's errorElement and your own error boundary, and keep the layout on screen when a page crashes
  • Move the focus and the scroll position after a page change so every visitor knows where they are
Key ideas
  • A router changes the address with the History API and renders the matching page without loading the app again. The server must still answer every address with index.html (a SPA fallback, Lesson 10.2).
  • Data mode: the routes are an array of typed route objects passed to createBrowserRouter, rendered by RouterProvider from react-router/dom. One package: react-router (never react-router-dom 7).
  • A layout route renders <Outlet /> where the child page goes; an index route is the page at its parent's address; a pathless route groups children (the HQ's errorElement); * catches the rest.
  • Link takes to and renders a real <a href>; NavLink adds aria-current="page", which is also what to style; Button as={Link} works with the polymorphic Button.
  • useParams values are string | undefined: narrow them; when only the parameter changes, the route stays the same, so key the page's state by the parameter.
  • After a navigation the app moves the focus to <main> and <ScrollRestoration /> handles the scroll; the title comes from useDocumentTitle.
Worked example: the route list, checked

The lesson's mistake in a scratch file: an index route with pages of its own. The route list is typed, so the compiler stops it:

import { createBrowserRouter } from 'react-router'
import { EventsPage } from './pages/EventsPage.tsx'
import { NewEventPage } from './pages/NewEventPage.tsx'

// An index route is the page AT its parent's address: it cannot have pages of its own
export const router = createBrowserRouter([
  {
    index: true,
    element: <EventsPage />,
    children: [{ path: 'new', element: <NewEventPage /> }],
  },
])
npx tsc -b
src/Scratch.tsx:7:3 - error TS2322: Type '{ index: true; element: JSX.Element; children: [{ path: string; element: JSX.Element; }]; }' is not assignable to type 'RouteObject'.
  Types of property 'children' are incompatible.
    Type '[{ path: string; element: Element; }]' is not assignable to type 'undefined'.

  7   {
      ~
  8     index: true,
    ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
...
 10     children: [{ path: 'new', element: <NewEventPage /> }],
    ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
 11   },
    ~~~


Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:7

Read it from the bottom up: children must be undefined, because index: true picked the index member of the RouteObject union. An index route is the page AT its parent's address; it cannot have pages of its own. The HQ's real list puts the pages under a route with no path, whose error element keeps the layout on screen when a page crashes:

shoreline-hq/src/App.tsx
// Every address the HQ knows, and the page it shows there
const router = createBrowserRouter([
  {
    path: '/',
    element: <AppLayout />,
    children: [
      {
        // A route without a path: it adds the error page and keeps the layout around it
        errorElement: <PageCrashed />,
        children: [
          { index: true, element: <HomePage /> },
          { path: 'events', element: <EventsPage /> },
          { path: 'events/new', element: <NewEventPage /> },
          { path: 'events/:eventId', element: <EventPage /> },
          { path: 'sites', element: <SitesPage /> },
          { path: 'sign-in', element: <SignInPage /> },
          { path: '*', element: <NotFoundPage /> },

import { BrowserRouter, Route, Routes } from 'react-router'
import { EventsPage } from './pages/EventsPage.tsx'
import { HomePage } from './pages/HomePage.tsx'

// Declarative mode: the same routes as JSX. Fine for small apps, but no loaders or actions.
export function DeclarativeApp() {
  return (
    <BrowserRouter>
      <Routes>
        <Route index element={<HomePage />} />
        <Route path="events" element={<EventsPage />} />
      </Routes>
    </BrowserRouter>
  )
}

6.2

Search Params, Nested Routes and Typed Navigation

By the end you can
  • Decide which state belongs in the address and which belongs in components
  • Read search params with useSearchParams and turn their string | null values into your own types with a type predicate
  • Change search params without losing the others, and choose between a new history entry and replace (plus flushSync for a text box)
  • Nest routes so a child page renders inside its parent's <Outlet />, with an index route for "nothing chosen yet"
  • Build relative links that keep the current search, and page through a list with links instead of buttons
  • Type route parameters with useParams<'name'>() and explain what href() and generatePath() check in data mode
Key ideas
  • State people want to keep, share or go Back to (filters, a search, a page number, the open item) belongs in the address; private or temporary state stays in components; secrets never.
  • useSearchParams() returns a URLSearchParams and a setter. Every value is string | null: narrow it with a type predicate (readParam), never with as.
  • Writing: change one name on a COPY of the current params (withParam), leave defaults out of the address; choices push (Back undoes them), typing replaces; a text box needs flushSync: true.
  • Nested routes: a route's children render at that route's <Outlet />; an index child is "nothing chosen yet"; the parent stays mounted (no list refetch).
  • Links resolve relative to their route. { pathname, search } keeps the query, { search } keeps the path. Pages of a list are links, so they can be shared and Back works.
  • Typed navigation: useParams<'name'>() checks the names you read, generatePath the names you build with; in data mode href() checks nothing. Hiding a page is courtesy; the API's 401 and 403 are the protection.
Worked example: every value is a string or null

The address only has text. The obvious line does not compile:

import { useSearchParams } from 'react-router'
import type { StatusFilter } from './components/EventFilters.tsx'

export function StatusLine() {
  const [searchParams] = useSearchParams()
  const status: StatusFilter = searchParams.get('status') ?? 'all'
  return <p>Status: {status}</p>
}
src/Scratch.tsx:6:9 - error TS2322: Type 'string' is not assignable to type 'StatusFilter'.
Found 1 error in src/Scratch.tsx:6

So the HQ checks the text with a generic helper that takes a type predicate. T is inferred from the predicate (isEventStatus gives EventStatus), and ?? 'all' supplies the default:

shoreline-hq/src/utils/searchParams.ts
export function readParam<T extends string>(
  params: URLSearchParams,
  name: string,
  isT: (value: string) => value is T,
): T | undefined {
  const value = params.get(name)
  return value !== null && isT(value) ? value : undefined
}

Its partner withParam changes one name on a COPY and removes an empty value, so defaults stay out of the address. Run on awkward values:

"" -> 1
"page=2" -> 2
"page=zero" -> 1
"page=2.5" -> 1
"page=0" -> 1
q=pelican | q=pelican&status=completed

Glossary

TermMeaning
router, client-side routingThe piece that changes the page when the address changes, without loading the app again
History APIThe browser's way to change the address and the Back/Forward list without a page load
data modeReact Router with route objects in createBrowserRouter([...]); needed for loaders and actions (Lesson 6.3)
route object, RouteObjectOne { path, element, children, errorElement, index } entry; a union of an index and a non-index route
layout route, <Outlet />A route whose element frames its children; the child page renders at the Outlet
index routeThe page at its parent's own address ("nothing chosen yet" in a nested route)
pathless routeA route with no path: it groups children so they share something (the HQ's error element)
catch-all route *Matches any address no other route matches: the not-found page
URL parameter, useParamsA :name segment of a path and the Hook that reads it (string | undefined)
Link, NavLink, aria-currentA router link (to); one that marks itself current with aria-current="page"
errorElementWhat a route renders instead of its page when something inside throws while rendering
SPA fallbackA host rule that answers every address with index.html (Lesson 10.2)
query string, search paramsEverything after the ?: name and value pairs, read with URLSearchParams
useSearchParams, setSearchParamsThe Hook that returns the current search params and a function that navigates to new ones
push, replaceAdd a history entry (Back undoes it) / overwrite the current one (Back skips it)
flushSync (router option)Apply this one update right away instead of as a transition: needed for a text box
nested routeA child route that renders inside its parent page, at the parent's Outlet
generatePath, href()Build a path from a pattern and values; generatePath checks the names in every mode, href only in framework mode

Lesson

6.3

Data Loading and Protected Routes

By the end you can
  • Explain the difference between rendering then fetching and loading then rendering, and when each fits
  • Write typed route loaders and read their data with useLoaderData<typeof loader>()
  • Turn failures while loading into route answers with data(), and show them with useRouteError and isRouteErrorResponse
  • Protect routes with guard loaders that redirect to sign-in with a safe ?next= address, and answer 403 to people without the right role
  • Handle a form with a route action, <Form>, useActionData and a redirect
  • Keep a session that React and the router both read, with useSyncExternalStore, and sign out automatically on a 401
Key ideas
  • Load then render: a loader runs when someone navigates to its route; the router shows the page once it finishes, and the old page stays meanwhile. useNavigation().state is 'loading' (or 'submitting' for an action).
  • Typed data: useLoaderData<typeof loader>() with an import type; without the type argument the data is any. Loaders live in their own .ts files.
  • Route answers: throw data(message, { status, statusText }) for expected failures and redirect() to send people elsewhere; one errorElement reads them with useRouteError() (unknown) and isRouteErrorResponse.
  • Await or a promise: await what the page cannot do without; return a promise for what can follow, and use() it inside Suspense.
  • Guards are loaders: guests get a redirect with a safe ?next= (only paths inside the app), the wrong role gets a 403 answer; a pathless route guards many pages; parent and child loaders run at the same time. The API's 401 and 403 remain the lock.
  • A store outside React (readSession, saveSession, onSessionChange) read with useSyncExternalStore; a route action handles the sign-in <Form>, returns why a try failed, throws a redirect on success.
Worked example: what each Hook returns, and a guard

The lesson's trick: ask TypeScript what each Hook returns by putting the result where a number is expected.

import { useActionData, useLoaderData } from 'react-router'
import type { signInAction } from './auth/signInAction.ts'
import type { eventLoader, sitesLoader } from './pages/loaders.ts'

// Ask TypeScript what each Hook returns: put the answer where a number is expected
export function Scratch() {
  const sites: number = useLoaderData<typeof sitesLoader>()
  const page: number = useLoaderData<typeof eventLoader>()
  const result: number = useActionData<typeof signInAction>()
  const untyped: number = useLoaderData()
  return <p>{sites}{page}{result}{untyped}</p>
}
npx tsc -b

          ~~~~~

8   const page: number = useLoaderData<typeof eventLoader>()
          ~~~~

src/Scratch.tsx:9:9 - error TS2322: Type 'SignInResult | undefined' is not assignable to type 'number'.
  Type 'undefined' is not assignable to type 'number'.

          ~~~~~~


Found 3 errors in the same file, starting at: src/Scratch.tsx:7

Three exact types, and NOTHING for line 10: without a type argument useLoaderData() is any. The loader that makes the first line work is three lines long; the router calls it before the page renders:

shoreline-hq/src/pages/loaders.ts
/** /sites: the six beach sites */
export function sitesLoader({ request }: LoaderFunctionArgs) {
  // request.signal: leaving the page before the answer arrives cancels the request
  return getSites(request.signal)
}

And the guard: a loader whose job is to throw. A redirect for guests (with the way back in ?next=), a 403 answer for the wrong role:

shoreline-hq/src/auth/guards.ts
/** For pages with contact details and planning tools: guests sign in first, volunteers are turned away */
export function organizersOnly({ request }: LoaderFunctionArgs) {
  const session = readSession()
  if (session === null) {
    // Remember the page (and its ?search) so the sign-in page can send people back to it
    const { pathname, search } = new URL(request.url)
    throw redirect(`/sign-in?next=${encodeURIComponent(pathname + search)}`)
  }
  if (session.user.role !== 'organizer') {
    const message = "This page has the crew's contact details and planning tools, so only organizers can open it."
    throw data(message, { status: 403, statusText: 'Organizers only' })
  }
  return null
}

P2
Predict each line Node prints, then run it. (The stage's nextPath on what a ?next= might hold (run with Node, it needs only URL).)
// nextPath, copied from src/auth/guards.ts: it needs nothing but URL
export function nextPath(url: URL): string {
  const next = url.searchParams.get('next')
  if (next === null || !next.startsWith('/') || next.startsWith('//') || next.startsWith('/\\')) {
    return '/'
  }
  return next
}

// What the guard puts in ?next=: an address inside an address, so it is encoded
console.log(encodeURIComponent('/volunteers?skill=first-aid'))

for (const next of ['/volunteers?skill=first-aid', 'events', '//example.com', 'https://elsewhere.example', '/\\elsewhere.example']) {
  const url = new URL(`http://localhost:5173/sign-in?next=${encodeURIComponent(next)}`)
  console.log(next.padEnd(28), '->', nextPath(url))
}

  × react-hooks(rules-of-hooks): React Hook "useAuth" is called in function "organizersOnly" that is neither a React function component nor a custom React Hook function. React component names must start with an uppercase letter. React Hook names must start with the word "use".

Part 2 Project: Basalt Peak Climbing Gym

Your instructor hands out labs/projects/part2-basalt-peak-routes/: BRIEF.md (the client's email, R1 to R6, the setup, the milestones, the rules, the hand-in), TASKS.md (the TODOs in order), RUBRIC.md and starter/. The client:

From Dana Okonkwo-Reyes, head route setter

Hi there! I'm Dana Okonkwo-Reyes, head route setter at Basalt Peak Climbing Gym in Millbrook. We reset our four walls every week, and right now the only way to know what is up is the whiteboard by the front desk (which nobody can read from the parking lot). Climbers keep asking us two things: what is on each wall this week, and where the V4s are. We would love a small web app: the routes by wall and grade, a page for each route that we can text to people, and a way for us setters to log a new route from a phone while we are still on the ladder. Only setters should be able to log routes; the front-desk staff can sign in and look, but please don't let them log anything. Our volunteer developer already built us a tiny API for the data (it is in the folder). Thanks a ton! Dana Okonkwo-Reyes, head route setter

Glossary

TermMeaning
render then fetch / load then renderThe page fetches after it appears ("Loading…") / the router fetches before it shows the page
loader, LoaderFunctionArgsA function on a route that the router calls on navigation, with params and the request (url, signal)
useLoaderData<typeof loader>()Reads the route's loader data, typed from the loader; any without the type argument
useNavigation()The navigation in progress: 'idle', 'loading' (loaders) or 'submitting' (an action)
data() (route answer)Thrown from a loader or action: a status, a status text and a message for the error page
redirect()Thrown from a loader or action: the router goes to another address before anything renders
useRouteError, isRouteErrorResponseWhat the error element reads (unknown), and the predicate that narrows it to a route answer
deferred dataA promise in loader data that the router does not wait for; the page use()s it inside Suspense
guard, protected routeA loader that decides who may open a page, and the routes below it
?next=, open redirectThe way back after sign-in; sending people to any address it names (never do it: only paths inside the app)
401 / 403"Who are you?" (sign in) / "I know who you are, and no"
shouldRevalidateTells the router when it may skip a route's loader (the HQ: when only the search changed)
action, <Form>, useActionDataA route function for a submitted form; the router's form; the action's last result
external store, useSyncExternalStoreA value outside React with a subscribe function; the Hook that reads it and re-renders on change
automatic sign-outrequest() forgets the session when the API answers 401 to a request that carried a token

A1
An effect fetches one cleanup with getEvent(eventId, controller.signal), lists [eventId] as its dependencies and returns () => controller.abort(). What does that cleanup function protect against? (1 point)
A) Nothing in the browser: it only matters in tests
B) A slow answer for the previous eventId arriving late and overwriting the page after the id changed (and a request left running after the component is gone)
C) The API answering with a 500
D) Strict Mode running the effect twice in production
A2
tsc prints this for the component below. Which change fixes it and keeps the effect correct? (1 point)
import { useEffect, useState } from 'react'
import { getSites } from './api/client.ts'
import type { Site } from './api/types.ts'

export function SiteCount() {
  const [sites, setSites] = useState<Site[]>([])
  useEffect(async () => {
    setSites(await getSites())
  }, [])
  return <p>{sites.length} beach sites</p>
}
src/Review.tsx:7:13 - error TS2345: Argument of type '() => Promise<void>' is not assignable to parameter of type 'EffectCallback'.
Found 1 error in src/Review.tsx:7
A) Put // @ts-expect-error above useEffect
B) Return the promise instead: useEffect(() => getSites().then(setSites), [])
C) Change the dependency array to [sites]
D) Keep the effect a plain function: call getSites(controller.signal).then(...) (or an async function declared inside it) and return a cleanup that aborts
A3
The HQ's useApi(key, load) lists key in its effect's dependencies but reads load through useEffectEvent. Why? (1 point)
A) A new key means new data, so the effect must run again; the caller's load is usually an inline arrow, a new function on every render, which would restart the effect (and the request) on every render
B) useEffectEvent makes the request faster
C) Hooks may not take functions as arguments
D) TypeScript cannot type load any other way
A4
tsc prints this for a search box whose button moves the focus into the input. Which fix is right? (1 point)
import { useRef } from 'react'

export function SearchBox() {
  const inputRef = useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null)
  function focusSearch() {
    inputRef.current.focus()
  }
  return (
    <>
      <input ref={inputRef} aria-label="Search cleanups" />
      <button type="button" onClick={focusSearch}>Search</button>
    </>
  )
}
src/Review.tsx:6:5 - error TS18047: 'inputRef.current' is possibly 'null'.
Found 1 error in src/Review.tsx:6
A) useRef<HTMLInputElement>(), without the null
B) Keep the input element in useState instead of a ref
C) inputRef.current?.focus(), or check inputRef.current !== null first
D) (inputRef.current as HTMLInputElement).focus()
A5
A component reads its data with use(promise) inside a <Suspense> boundary. Where must that promise come from? (1 point)
A) From a call such as getEvent(id) in the component's body, so every render has fresh data
B) From a try block around use, so errors are caught
C) From an effect's cleanup function
D) From something stable that hands back the SAME promise for the same id: a cache (Lesson 4.4's eventPromises) or a route loader (Lesson 6.3)

A timer's reducer ends its switch with default: return assertNever(action), and tsc prints the error below. What does it tell you, and what is the fix? (2 points)
import { assertNever } from './utils/assertNever.ts'

interface TimerState {
  running: boolean
  seconds: number
}

type TimerAction =
  | { type: 'start' }
  | { type: 'tick' }
  | { type: 'pause' }
  | { type: 'reset' }

export function timerReducer(state: TimerState, action: TimerAction): TimerState {
  switch (action.type) {
    case 'start':
      return { ...state, running: true }
    case 'tick':
      return state.running ? { ...state, seconds: state.seconds + 1 } : state
    case 'pause':
      return { ...state, running: false }
    default:
      return assertNever(action)
  }
}

Lesson

7.1

TanStack Query

By the end you can
  • Explain what server state is, and what a cache adds to fetching it
  • Set up a QueryClient and write typed queryOptions factories with keys that include everything the answer depends on
  • Read a query's result with useQuery, narrowing it with isSuccess, and keep the last list on screen with placeholderData
  • Choose staleTime and retry settings and explain the requests they cause
  • Fill the cache from route loaders and read it with useSuspenseQuery, with a Try again that really tries again
Key ideas
  • Server state is borrowed: the API owns cleanups, sites and the crew; the HQ holds a copy that ages. Client state (a dialog, the search text, the undo history) belongs to the browser.
  • The logbook: a QueryClient (the cache) keeps each answer under a query key; a query function fetches it (with an AbortSignal). Equal keys share one entry and one request.
  • The key rule: the key holds everything the answer depends on (filters, page, who asks), general to specific, the viewer last: ['events', 'list', search, viewer]. Then ['events'] reaches every list and detail.
  • queryOptions factories keep key and function together and carry the data type to every Hook and loader: no type arguments.
  • A result is a union tagged by status: pending / error / success. isSuccess narrows data; isFetching is a separate question (a background refresh). placeholderData: keepPreviousData keeps the last list while a new key loads.
  • Fresh, stale, retried: staleTime (the HQ: 30 s) decides when to ask again; gcTime (5 min) when to forget; retry which failures deserve another try (never a 4xx). Loaders: queryClient.query({ ...q, staleTime: 'static' }), pages: useSuspenseQuery, boundaries: onRetry={reset}.
Worked example: what the query Hooks give back

The lesson's Scratch.tsx, which hands each query result to a function that wants a number:

import { useQuery, useSuspenseQuery } from '@tanstack/react-query'
import { eventQuery, eventsQuery } from './api/queries.ts'

// What do the query Hooks give back? Hand each result to a function that wants a number.
function show(value: number) {
  return value
}

export function Scratch() {
  const list = useQuery(eventsQuery('perPage=50', 'guest'))
  const cleanup = useSuspenseQuery(eventQuery('e08', 'guest'))
  show(list.data)
  show(cleanup.data)
  if (list.isSuccess) {
    show(list.data)
  }
  return null
}

npx tsc -b on the finished Lesson 7.1 code:

src/Scratch.tsx:12:8 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'Page<CleanupEvent> | undefined' is not assignable to parameter of type 'number'.
  Type 'undefined' is not assignable to type 'number'.

12   show(list.data)
          ~~~~~~~~~

src/Scratch.tsx:13:8 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'CleanupEventDetail' is not assignable to parameter of type 'number'.

13   show(cleanup.data)
          ~~~~~~~~~~~~

src/Scratch.tsx:15:10 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'Page<CleanupEvent>' is not assignable to parameter of type 'number'.

15     show(list.data)
            ~~~~~~~~~


Found 3 errors in the same file, starting at: src/Scratch.tsx:12

Read it in three moves. Line 12: useQuery's data is Page<CleanupEvent> | undefined: there is no data until the query succeeds. Line 15: inside if (list.isSuccess) the same expression is Page<CleanupEvent>: the check narrowed the union. Line 13: useSuspenseQuery's data is CleanupEventDetail, never undefined: it suspends instead of returning without data. And nowhere a type argument: every type came from the factory's query function.

Glossary

TermMeaning
server state / client stateData the API owns and the app copies / data only this browser knows
TanStack QueryThe library that keeps server state in a shared cache (many people still call it React Query)
QueryClientThe cache itself, created once outside React; QueryClientProvider hands it to every component
query keyAn array that labels a cache entry; it holds everything the answer depends on
query functionThe function that fetches an entry; it gets a signal that is aborted when nobody needs the answer
queryOptionsPuts a key and a function together and keeps their types (the HQ's factories)
useQueryReads a query in a component: a result tagged by status (pending, error, success)
isFetchingA request is running right now (also during a background refresh of good data)
placeholderData: keepPreviousDataKeep the last answer on screen while a new key loads; isPlaceholderData says so
fresh / stale, staleTimeYoung enough to use without asking / shown but refetched; the time that separates them
gcTimeHow long an unused entry stays in memory (was cacheTime in version 4)
retryWhich failures to try again, and how often (the HQ: never 4xx, at most 2 more)
useSuspenseQueryReads a query and suspends until there is data: its data is never undefined
queryClient.queryFills (or reads) an entry from outside a component; with staleTime: 'static' any cached copy counts
useQueryErrorResetBoundaryGives reset, which lets queries below an error boundary try again
indexed access typeImpactTotals['byMonth'][number]: the type of one item of a property's array

Lessons

7.2

Mutations

By the end you can
  • Send typed POST and PATCH requests and check what comes back
  • Write mutationOptions factories and run them with useMutation, mutate and mutateAsync
  • Invalidate the cache by key after a change, and explain which entries a change makes out of date
  • Show an API's 400 fieldErrors under the fields they belong to
  • Decide when an update should be optimistic, and write one with onMutate, a rollback in onError and a refetch in onSettled
Key ideas
  • A mutation changes server data (POST, PATCH, DELETE). It runs only when you call it, its answer is not cached, and it is not retried by default (a retried POST could save twice).
  • mutationOptions factories (the twin of queryOptions) carry the types: mutate takes the mutation function's parameter, data is its promise's value. mutate starts and moves on; mutateAsync returns a promise to await (and catch).
  • Invalidation: after a change, ask "what could this have changed?" and call invalidateQueries with the general key (['events']); entries on screen refetch now, the others when needed. Return the promise from onSuccess.
  • When the API says no: a 400 with fieldErrors keyed by the form's field names goes straight under the fields; other failures become one sentence (describeError).
  • Wait or optimistic: big or hard to take back → wait; small, frequent, easy to undo → optimistic: onMutate (cancelQueries, write the cache, return the old value), onError (roll back), onSettled (refetch the truth).
  • Server state leaves the reducer: the marks live in the API (read through the cache); the desk's reducer keeps only the undo history of accepted changes.
Worked example: a rollback TypeScript insists on

The rollback in markMutation, written as if onMutate always answered:

onError: (_error, { signupId }, saved) => {
  setStatus(signupId, saved.before)
},

npx tsc -b on the finished Lesson 7.2 code with that line:

src/api/mutations.ts:68:27 - error TS18048: 'saved' is possibly 'undefined'.

68       setStatus(signupId, saved.before)
                             ~~~~~

src/api/mutations.ts:68:27 - error TS2345: Argument of type 'SignupStatus | undefined' is not assignable to parameter of type 'SignupStatus'.
  Type 'undefined' is not assignable to type 'SignupStatus'.

68       setStatus(signupId, saved.before)
                             ~~~~~~~~~~~~


Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/api/mutations.ts:68

Two errors at the same spot. saved is possibly undefined: onError also runs when onMutate itself failed, before it returned anything. before may be undefined: the sign-up was not in the cached roster. The fix answers both: if (saved?.before !== undefined) { setStatus(signupId, saved.before) }. The types turned a rare failure into a line you cannot forget.

7.3

Client State

By the end you can
  • Decide where a piece of state belongs: the query cache, the URL, a component, a reducer, or an app-wide store
  • Create a typed Zustand store with state and actions, and read it with selectors that re-render only what changed
  • Keep a store across reloads with the persist middleware, and check the saved data before trusting it
  • Use a store outside React (getState, subscribe) for page-wide effects like a theme
  • Read Redux Toolkit code (slices, typed hooks, a Provider) and compare it with Zustand on size, code and tooling
Key ideas
  • Where state lives: server data → the query cache; what a link should reproduce → the URL; one component's business → useState or a reducer; the session → the session store; app-wide preferences that outlive the tab → an app store. Keep state as close as possible to where it is used (colocation).
  • Zustand: create<UiState>()(…) makes a store of values and actions outside React; set merges and never changes the old object. The extra () lets you give the type while TypeScript infers the middleware's.
  • Selectors decide re-renders: a component re-renders when its selector's answer changes (Object.is). Select one value or a derived boolean; a selector that builds an object needs useShallow (or React gives up).
  • persist saves to localStorage under a name; what comes back is unknown: a merge checks each field and falls back to the default.
  • Outside React: useUiStore.getState() and subscribe((state, previous) => …): the theme goes on <html data-theme> from main.tsx.
  • Redux Toolkit (slices, PayloadAction, typed Hooks, a Provider) solves the same problem with named actions and DevTools; reach for it when the codebase already uses it.
Worked example: typed selectors

The lesson's Scratch.tsx reads two values from the display store with no type arguments, then makes two mistakes:

import { useUiStore } from './state/uiStore.ts'

export function Scratch() {
  const density = useUiStore((state) => state.density)
  const setTheme = useUiStore((state) => state.setTheme)
  const small = density === 'cozy'
  return <button onClick={() => setTheme('purple')}>{small ? 'Small' : 'Large'}</button>
}

npx tsc -b on the finished Lesson 7.3 code:

src/Scratch.tsx:6:17 - error TS2367: This comparison appears to be unintentional because the types '"compact" | "roomy"' and '"cozy"' have no overlap.

6   const small = density === 'cozy'
                  ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~

src/Scratch.tsx:7:42 - error TS2345: Argument of type '"purple"' is not assignable to parameter of type '"dark" | "light" | "system"'.

7   return <button onClick={() => setTheme('purple')}>{small ? 'Small' : 'Large'}</button>
                                           ~~~~~~~~


Found 2 errors in the same file, starting at: src/Scratch.tsx:6

The store's interface flows into every selector: density is "compact" | "roomy", so comparing it with 'cozy' can never be true (TS2367), and setTheme accepts only the three themes (TS2345). These are Module 2's checks, now reaching across the whole app through one store.

Glossary

TermMeaning
mutationA change to server data (POST, PATCH, DELETE), run when you call it, not cached
mutationOptionsThe mutation twin of queryOptions: one factory per kind of change, with its types
useMutationRuns a mutation in a component: mutate, mutateAsync, status flags, data, error, variables
invalidationMarking cached entries stale after a change (invalidateQueries by key prefix)
optimistic updateShowing a change before the API answers, and rolling it back if it says no
onMutate / onError / onSettledBefore the request (return what to put back) / after a failure / after either
cancelQueriesStops refetches already on their way, so they cannot overwrite an optimistic change
setQueryDataChanges a cache entry by hand (typed through the factory's key)
client state, colocationState only this browser knows / keep state as close as possible to where it is used
Zustand, store, selectorA small app-wide state library / its values and actions / a function from the state to the part a component needs
useShallowCompares an object selector's values instead of the object itself
persist, mergeZustand middleware that saves to localStorage / where you check what comes back
light-dark(), color-schemeA CSS function that picks a light or dark value / the property that decides which
Redux Toolkit, sliceThe official modern Redux / one part of the store with its reducers and actions

Lessons

8.1

React Hook Form + Zod

By the end you can
  • Write a Zod 4 schema that is both a TypeScript type and the checks behind it, with messages people understand
  • Tell a schema's input type from its output type (z.input, z.output) and give useForm the right generics
  • Build a form with React Hook Form: register, handleSubmit, formState and zodResolver
  • Show the API's 400 field errors under the right fields with setError, and a form-wide message with a root error
  • Decide when a form needs a library and when React 19's form actions are enough, and say why
Key ideas
  • A Zod schema is one value that checks data and gives TypeScript types: z.object, z.string().trim().min(5, …), z.enum, .refine, z.coerce.number<string>(), an object refine with path and when.
  • Two types: z.input (what the boxes hold: text) and z.output (what a successful check gives: numbers). z.infer is the output.
  • safeParse returns a union on success; z.flattenError(error).fieldErrors gives field → messages.
  • React Hook Form: useForm({ resolver: zodResolver(schema), defaultValues }) with no type arguments; register(name), handleSubmit(save), formState.errors, isSubmitting, setError.
  • The API's 400: setError each key a type predicate accepts (the first one focused) and 'root.server' for the form-wide message. The form is a courtesy; the API is the lock.
  • Two forms, one schema: Plan a cleanup keeps useActionState with safeParse; the edit form uses RHF. Zod stays out of client.ts for the bundle's sake (Lesson 10.1).
Worked example: the schema with Node

The lesson's quick look at the schema, run with Node (node src/try-schema.ts):

// A quick look at the schema with Node. Delete this file when you are done.
import { z } from 'zod'
import { eventFormSchema } from './api/schemas.ts'

const typed = {
  title: '  Dune Grass Cleanup ',
  siteId: 'north-jetty',
  date: '2099-06-14',
  startTime: '09:00',
  endTime: '11:30',
  capacity: '12',
  meetingPoint: 'North jetty steps',
  description: '',
}
console.log(eventFormSchema.parse(typed))

const result = eventFormSchema.safeParse({ ...typed, title: 'Dune', capacity: 'lots', endTime: '08:00' })
if (!result.success) {
  console.log(z.flattenError(result.error).fieldErrors)
}
{
  title: 'Dune Grass Cleanup',
  siteId: 'north-jetty',
  date: '2099-06-14',
  startTime: '09:00',
  endTime: '11:30',
  capacity: 12,
  meetingPoint: 'North jetty steps',
  description: ''
}
{
  title: [ 'Use at least 5 characters for the title.' ],
  capacity: [ 'Capacity must be a whole number from 1 to 200.' ],
  endTime: [ 'The end time must be after the start time.' ]
}

Read it twice. The first object is the checked data: the title lost its spaces, capacity is 12, a number (the output type). The second is z.flattenError(...).fieldErrors: one list of messages per field, the shape the HQ's FieldError already shows, and the end-time message arrives with the others because the object refine has when.

8.2

Complex Forms

By the end you can
  • Describe a list inside a form with a Zod array schema, including a rule about the whole list that puts its message on the right row
  • Build rows people can add and remove with React Hook Form's useFieldArray, with stable keys, typed field paths and sensible focus
  • Build an error summary that takes the focus after a failed save and links to every field with a problem
  • Upload a file with FormData: check it in the browser, send it without breaking the request, and explain the API's 413 and 415 answers
  • Save with PUT and refresh every cached answer the save changes
  • Decide when a long form should become steps, and how to check one step at a time
Key ideas
  • A list in a schema: z.array(rowSchema); counts check the text (regex) and then transform(Number): an empty box is a message, not 0.
  • A rule about the list: .superRefine with context.addIssue({ path: [index, 'category'], … }) puts the message on the repeated row; when lets it run while a count is wrong.
  • useFieldArray: fields (key by field.id), append(row, { focusName }), remove(index) then focus something; paths like items.${index}.count are typed.
  • Error summary: key={submitCount}, takes the focus, links that focus each field; shouldFocusError: false; messages under the fields stay.
  • Uploads: check type and size first (accept is a hint); FormData without a Content-Type header; the API answers 413/415; upload first, keep the id.
  • Save with PUT, write the answer with setQueryData, invalidate what else changed. Steps only when the parts are separate tasks.
Worked example: a rule on the right row

The lesson's report check with Node (node src/try-impact.ts): a duplicate row and an empty box.

// The report schema with Node: a duplicate row and an empty box
import { z } from 'zod'
import { impactReportSchema } from './api/schemas.ts'

const result = impactReportSchema.safeParse({
  volunteersAttended: '11',
  bags: '',
  weightKg: '18.5',
  items: [
    { category: 'plastic-bottles', count: '40' },
    { category: 'glass', count: 'lots' },
    { category: 'plastic-bottles', count: '3' },
  ],
  notes: '',
})
if (!result.success) {
  for (const issue of result.error.issues) {
    console.log(issue.path.join('.'), '→', issue.message)
  }
  console.log(z.flattenError(result.error).fieldErrors)
}
bags → Bags must be a whole number from 0 to 500.
items.1.count → Count must be a whole number from 0 to 100000.
items.2.category → Plastic bottles is already in the list: add to its count.
{
  bags: [ 'Bags must be a whole number from 0 to 500.' ],
  items: [
    'Count must be a whole number from 0 to 100000.',
    'Plastic bottles is already in the list: add to its count.'
  ]
}

Each issue's path says exactly where it belongs: items.2.category is row 3's menu (rows count from 0). The flattened object at the end lost the rows: for nested data, use the paths (React Hook Form's resolver does).

Lesson

9.1

Testing Components

By the end you can
  • Explain what unit, component and end-to-end tests each check in the HQ, and why tests should describe what people see rather than how the code works inside
  • Set up Vitest, jsdom and React Testing Library in a Vite project, sharing the app's own vite.config.ts
  • Write unit tests for functions and Zod schemas with describe, it, expect, it.each and typed sample-data factories
  • Render components and find elements the way people do, by role and accessible name, choosing between getBy, queryBy and findBy
  • Build a typed renderWithProviders helper (cache, sign-in, router) and reset the app's module-level state between tests
  • Read a failing test's output and decide whether the test or the app is wrong
Key ideas
  • Three sizes: unit (a function), component (a component in jsdom's pretend page), end-to-end (the whole app in a real browser, Lesson 9.3). Most of a healthy suite is the first two.
  • Vitest reads vite.config.ts: the same plugins as the app (the React Compiler included); environment: 'jsdom', setupFiles, include. It strips types without checking them: tsc -b still checks your tests.
  • describe / it / expect, Arrange-Act-Assert; toBe for single values, toEqual for objects and arrays; it.each([...] as const) for tables.
  • See every test fail once, then ask: is the test wrong, or the app?
  • Queries: by role and name first, then label, then text, test id last; getBy (here now), queryBy (not here), findBy (soon). A query that cannot find something is often an accessibility bug.
  • A render helper puts the app's providers around a component; module state (cache, session, stores, storage) is reset after every test.
Worked example: a failure is information

The lesson's first test with Jul changed to July on purpose:

 FAIL  src/utils/format.test.ts > formatDate > shows a day of the crew calendar with its weekday
AssertionError: expected 'Sat, Jul 4, 2026' to be 'Sat, July 4, 2026' // Object.is equality
Expected: "Sat, July 4, 2026"
Received: "Sat, Jul 4, 2026"

Read it like a compiler error: the test (describe > it), the expected value, the received one, the line. Then decide: the app writes short month names on purpose, so the test is wrong. Change the app to long names instead and two tests fail: then the app is wrong. A red test is information, not a verdict on you.

Glossary

TermMeaning
unit / component / end-to-end testOne function / one component in a pretend page / the whole app in a real browser
VitestThe test runner built for Vite: reads vite.config.ts, runs *.test.ts(x)
jsdomA pretend browser page inside Node: elements, events, focus; no layout, no pictures
React Testing Libraryrender() and screen with queries that find elements the way people do
jest-domExtra matchers for elements (toBeInTheDocument, toHaveFocus); import @testing-library/jest-dom/vitest
matcherThe second half of a check: .toBe(x), .toEqual(x), .toHaveLength(n)
Arrange-Act-AssertSet up the input, run the code, check the result
it.eachOne test per row of a table; %s puts the row into the name
test factoryA function that returns complete, typed sample data (makeEvent(changes))
getBy / queryBy / findByHere now (throws) / not here (null) / soon (waits)
accessible nameWhat a screen reader announces with an element's role
setup fileRuns before each test file (setupFiles): jest-dom, cleanup, resets
render helperRenders a component inside the app's providers (renderWithProviders)
test isolationEach test starts from the same state, whatever ran before it

Lessons

9.2

Interactions and Async

By the end you can
  • Click, type, tab and upload in tests with user-event, and explain why each action is awaited
  • Answer the app's requests in tests with MSW 3 running the API's own code, reset it between tests and fail on requests nobody answers
  • Wait for data with findBy and waitFor, and replace one answer for one test with server.use(), typed with the API's own types
  • Test whole pages through the app's real routes, including loaders, redirects and the organizers-only guard
  • Test custom Hooks and TanStack Query mutations with renderHook, including an optimistic update and its rollback
Key ideas
  • user-event fires the events a person causes; userEvent.setup() before render; await every action.
  • MSW 3 catches requests inside Node; the HQ's handlers run shoreline-api's own core (src/mocks, never edited). onUnhandledFrame: 'error', api.reset() before each test, resetHandlers() after.
  • Waiting: findBy for elements the API brings; waitFor for things that are not elements; getBy after an awaited action that changed the page at once.
  • server.use(): one answer for one test, typed satisfies ApiErrorBody. Prefer the real rule (Weekend Cove really is full).
  • renderApp(route, session) renders the real routes (loaders, guards, redirects); recordRequests() proves what was not sent.
  • renderHook tests a Hook on its own (initialProps, rerender, unmount, a wrapper for providers); retries off in tests.
Worked example: a guard that still looks right

The guard rule has two halves: guests are sent to sign in, and they are sent away BEFORE any request goes out. With the guard line commented out, the screen still looks right; the test's request log does not:

AssertionError: expected [ 'GET /api/events/e08', …(1) ] to deeply equal []

- Expected
+ Received

- []
+ [
+   "GET /api/events/e08",
+   "GET /api/sites",

recordRequests() writes down every request MSW sees; started right before the action, it holds only what the action caused. "Nothing must happen" is a rule like any other, and only a test can keep it true.

9.3

End-to-End and Accessibility Tests

By the end you can
  • Set up Playwright so one command builds the HQ, starts or reuses shoreline-api and the preview server, and runs the tests in Chromium
  • Write end-to-end tests with locators by role and label, auto-waiting actions and web-first assertions, and read their failures
  • Keep tests that share one real server independent: a reset before every test, one test at a time, and a clean API afterwards
  • Run axe accessibility checks on the HQ's key pages in both themes, and explain what automated checks can and cannot find
  • Decide for any behavior whether it needs a unit, component or end-to-end test, and why most of a suite should not be end-to-end
Key ideas
  • End-to-end = the build in a real browser against the real server: bytes, pictures, colors, layout, reloads.
  • Playwright: fixtures ({ page }, { request }), locators by role and label (strict: one element or an error), actions that wait, web-first assertions that retry (await expect(...)).
  • One real server: reset before every test (POST /api/_reset), workers: 1, a globalTeardown that leaves the beach as you found it. It resets YOUR running API too.
  • axe (@axe-core/playwright, WCAG A/AA tags) in both themes after each page's heading: a floor, not a ceiling.
  • What to test where: many cases → unit; one screen, errors, nothing sent → component; only real together → one e2e journey.
  • First run can take a minute or two before "Running…"; npx playwright install chromium when it says Executable doesn't exist.
Worked example: one faint gray

One faint gray in the dark theme. Every light scan passes, every dark scan fails, and each failure names the rule (the lesson's card):

  ✘   8 e2e/accessibility.spec.ts:23:7 › dark theme › / has no axe violations (561ms)
...
  1) e2e/accessibility.spec.ts:23:7 › dark theme › / has no axe violations ─────────────────────────

    Error: expect(received).toEqual(expected) // deep equality

    - Expected  - 1
    + Received  + 3

    - Array []
    + Array [
    +   "color-contrast: Elements must meet minimum color contrast ratio thresholds (1)",
...
  7 failed
...
  7 passed (15.3s)

Nothing else would have caught it: tsc and oxlint do not read colors, jsdom does not compute them, and in light mode on your own screen the HQ looks the same.

  • labs/module-22/l9_3-end-to-end-and-accessibility-tests/TASKS.md; npm install once in starter, Chromium from Lesson 9.3, then npm run test:e2e (two TODOs).

Session 23: Performance and the Build

Your workbook for Session 23: the key ideas of Lesson 10.1, a worked example of who downloads what, the guided lab, the project checklist with your own numbers, the self-check and your exit ticket.

Lesson 10.1 Session 23 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Read Vite's build table: chunks, sizes, gzip, hashed names, the 500 kB warning.
  • Measure what each kind of visitor downloads, and look inside the bundle with a treemap.
  • Split with typed lazy routes and React.lazy; shape files with codeSplitting groups for caching.
  • Protect the result with a size budget, and write a typed, formula-safe CSV export.

Lesson

10.1

Performance and the Build

By the end you can
  • Read Vite 8's build output (chunks, sizes, gzip, hashed names) and explain what its 500 kB warning is telling you
  • Measure the JavaScript a visitor downloads, and find what is inside a bundle with a treemap
  • Split a React Router app with typed lazy routes, and use React.lazy with Suspense for a component
  • Shape the build's files with Rolldown's codeSplitting groups for better caching, without pulling lazy code back in
  • Protect the result with an end-to-end size budget, and add a typed, safe CSV export to the Impact page
Key ideas
  • The build (tsc -b && vite build, Rolldown): follows every import, drops unused code, minifies, writes hashed files to dist/. Two sizes: on disk (what the phone runs) and gzip (roughly what travels).
  • Measure, look, split, measure again: the Network tab on npm run preview (never the dev server), the treemap from npm run analyze.
  • Dynamic import makes a separate file. A route's lazy (function form, returns { Component }) downloads it with the loaders; React.lazy (needs { default }) downloads it when React renders: a waterfall.
  • Splitting is for speed, not for secrets: anyone can read every file; guards decide what a person sees, the API what anyone may do.
  • codeSplitting.groups decide which file a module lands in: a vendor file keeps its name across app changes (caching). A group that catches too much sends it to every visitor.
  • A budget test (no forms file, under 440 kB for a guest's tour) fails on any cause. CSV: RFC 4180 quoting, CRLF, and a ' in front of formula-looking text.
Worked example: who pays for what

Before the lesson, every visit downloads one file:

dist/assets/index-B-Vm3u1y.js                            581.69 kB │ gzip: 182.31 kB

After lazy routes and two groups, a guest's first visit loads index, vendor and a tiny runtime; organizer pages and the form libraries arrive only when an organizer opens them:

dist/assets/rolldown-runtime-WkwZ5CU-.js                   0.59 kB │ gzip:   0.36 kB
dist/assets/index-BGWJtCEI.js                             62.72 kB │ gzip:  21.44 kB
dist/assets/forms-wPpF5eqp.js                            127.33 kB │ gzip:  38.45 kB
dist/assets/vendor-DenlHpDj.js                           355.82 kB │ gzip: 111.15 kB

Change the HomePage tagline and build again: only index gets a new name; a returning visitor reuses the 356 kB vendor file from the cache:

# before the change
dist/assets/index-BGWJtCEI.js                             62.72 kB │ gzip:  21.44 kB
dist/assets/vendor-DenlHpDj.js                           355.82 kB │ gzip: 111.15 kB
# after changing the tagline
dist/assets/index-B0OsZI9M.js                             62.71 kB │ gzip:  21.44 kB
dist/assets/vendor-DenlHpDj.js                           355.82 kB │ gzip: 111.15 kB

Glossary

TermMeaning
bundle, chunkThe files the build writes / one of those JavaScript files
RolldownVite 8's bundler
content hashThe part of a file name that changes when its content changes (safe to cache forever)
gzipCompression on the way; the browser unpacks and runs the full size
treemapA picture of the bundle: each box's area is its size (npm run analyze)
dynamic importimport('./x.tsx'): a Promise of the module, and a separate file
lazy routeA route's lazy: async () => ({ Component }): the page's file loads with its loaders
React.lazyA component loaded the first time it renders; needs { default } and a Suspense boundary
waterfallRequests that wait for each other (data, then code)
codeSplitting.groupsRules that decide which file a module lands in (a vendor file, a forms file)
performance budgetA limit a test enforces (here: bytes for a guest's tour)
CSV injectionText that a spreadsheet would run as a formula; defused with a leading '

Lesson

10.2

Deploying

By the end you can
  • Explain the HQ's environments (development, tests, preview, demo, deployed) and how Vite modes and import.meta.env decide what each build contains
  • Build a demo version that runs shoreline-api's own code in the browser through MSW's service worker, without a byte of it reaching the normal build
  • Make deep links work on a static host with a _redirects fallback, and set caching and security headers with _headers
  • Write a GitHub Actions workflow that runs the type check, lint, unit tests, both builds and the Playwright suite against the real API on every push
  • Choose a host by reading its current terms, deploy a draft and then production with the Netlify CLI, and check the live site from the outside
Key ideas
  • Environments are builds: development, unit tests, e2e, demo, deployed. Vite replaces import.meta.env.MODE and every VITE_ value with text at build time; a branch that is always false disappears with its imports.
  • The demo build (npm run build:demo, mode demo) runs shoreline-api's own core in the browser through MSW's service worker: works on any static host, private sample data per visitor, resets on reload, a banner that says so.
  • A build decision belongs in the build: MODE, never the hostname.
  • Static hosts: public/_redirects /* /index.html 200 for deep links; public/_headers: /assets/* immutable for a year, index.html checked every visit, three security headers.
  • CI (GitHub Actions): one repository (the course folder), two jobs; npm ci, tsc -b, lint --deny-warnings, Vitest, both builds; then e2e against the real API with needs: checks.
  • Hosts' terms: Netlify Free 300 credits a month, 15 per production deploy: deploy by hand after green checks (draft, look, --prod).
Worked example: one source, two builds

The demo build carries the API's code and the worker:

dist/assets/vendor-DenlHpDj.js                           355.82 kB │ gzip: 111.15 kB
dist/assets/browser-urx2_hst.js                          429.68 kB │ gzip: 164.46 kB

The normal build of the same source has neither:

dist/assets/forms-wPpF5eqp.js                            127.33 kB │ gzip:  38.45 kB
dist/assets/vendor-DenlHpDj.js                           355.82 kB │ gzip: 111.15 kB

Both builds copy public/ into dist/, so the host's files are where the host reads them:

_headers  _redirects  assets  favicon.svg  images  index.html  mockServiceWorker.js

Glossary

TermMeaning
environmentOne place the code runs, with its own settings (and, for a front end, its own build)
Vite mode, import.meta.env.MODEdevelopment, production, test or any --mode name; replaced with text at build time
demo buildnpm run build:demo: the API's code runs in the visitor's browser through MSW
service workerA script the browser installs for a site; it can answer the site's requests
static host, CDNServes a folder of files over HTTPS from servers around the world; runs none of your code
SPA fallback/* /index.html 200: every address without a file gets the app's page
Cache-Control: immutableKeep this file; it never changes (safe for hashed file names)
continuous integration (CI)A computer runs the checks on every push and marks the change green or red
workflow, job, step, runnerGitHub Actions' YAML file / a group of steps on one machine / one command or action / the machine
needsOne job waits for another and is skipped if it fails
artifactA file a run keeps for download (the Playwright report on failure)
draft / production deployA temporary address to look at / the live address (15 credits on Netlify Free)
launch-readiness checklaunch_check.mjs: Git, repository, workflow, host files, remote, Netlify link, and the by-hand list

Session 25: Final Project Workshop and Next Steps

Your workbook for Session 25: the final project's start (brief, charter, types first, the starter, milestones and how it is scored), the advanced TypeScript you will meet next, the guided labs, predict-then-check practice, a self-check for each lesson, your final project plan and your exit ticket.

Lessons 11.1–11.2 Session 25 · 3 hours Name: ____________________

Session Goals

  • Choose a client brief (or size your own idea), write a charter with an honest out of scope, and plan the app on the worksheet from your journal.
  • Write domain types first: unions, null for "known to be empty", money in cents, computed values never stored, request types built from the domain types.
  • Read the advanced TypeScript libraries are made of (satisfies, mapped, conditional and branded types, declaration files) and write one of each.
  • Leave with milestone dates, a peer reviewer and a showcase slot, and know exactly how the final project and the course are completed.

Lessons

11.1

Final Project

By the end you can
  • Choose and size a client brief for a React + TypeScript app, and write a charter that says what is in scope and what is not
  • Plan the whole app on one worksheet, starting from your journal: domain types, routes, data layer, state placement, forms, tests by level, accessibility, a performance budget and the launch path
  • Write an app's domain types first, with unions and complete Record tables, and let the type checker find every place a change has to reach
  • Start from the final-project kit's starter and build Milestone 1: your types, sample data and a mock API with its rules and tests, committed with CI green
  • Turn about 25 hours of work into milestones with honest estimates, and define "done" with a rubric and a peer review
Key ideas
  • A brief, not a lesson plan: you choose a client (seven briefs, or your own idea within the size rules) and write a charter that says what is in scope and what is not, with a reason.
  • The worksheet is the crew lead's clipboard: thirteen sections in course order, most of them filled from your journal's "your own project" notes.
  • Types first: unions instead of strings, null for "known to be empty", money in whole cents (Cents), computed values never stored, request types with Pick / Partial.
  • Record tables keyed by a union make the type checker list every place a new value must reach; deleting the example Item turns npx tsc -b into a to-do list.
  • The starter is the HQ's tooling with a mock API in src/mocks that answers in tests, in npm run dev:demo and in the demo build; share RULES between form and API, not schemas.
  • Milestones turn about 25 hours into evenings: CI green in Milestone 1, a draft deploy in Milestone 2, a time log, a re-plan at 50 % over. Done = no rubric row at "not yet", checked by a peer.
Worked example: one new error code, followed by the compiler

The worked example adds one error code to the starter, 'conflict', for a 409 answer. The client keeps its codes in a Record<ErrorCode, true>, so the type checker hands over the one place that must learn about it:

npx tsc -b
src/api/client.ts:34:7 - error TS2741: Property 'conflict' is missing in type '{ validation_failed: true; not_found: true; server_error: true; }' but required in type 'Record<ErrorCode, true>'.

34 const errorCodes: Record<ErrorCode, true> = {
         ~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in src/api/client.ts:34

Add conflict: true to the table and npx tsc -b is silent again. With an array of codes it would have compiled and treated every 409 as unknown. That is "types first": change the description once, follow the list.

  • npx tsc -b (15 errors: your to-do) and npx vitest run (9 of the new tests fail with TODO).
  • Task 1: the types and the two label Records. Run npx tsc -b after each change.

11.2

Next Steps

By the end you can
  • Read the advanced TypeScript you will meet in libraries and team code (satisfies, mapped types, template literal types, conditional types with infer, branded types) and write a small example of each
  • Give an untyped package a declaration file instead of silencing the error, and choose satisfies over as when you want a value checked
  • Explain what a framework such as Next.js adds to a Vite single-page app, and what carries over to React Native and the volunteers' phone app on the same API and types
  • Work on a team with short-lived branches, a pull request template, CI and helpful code review, and keep a project current with release notes and npm outdated
  • Look back over the whole course and write a learning plan for the months after it
Key ideas
  • Library types compute types from types: Partial and Record are mapped types, ReturnType a conditional type with infer. Read them from the outside in, one level at a time.
  • satisfies checks a value against a type and keeps the value's own, more precise type; as trusts you and checks almost nothing; an annotation replaces the value's type.
  • Mapped types build a type from another's keys (as renames them), template literal types give strings a pattern, conditional types with infer take types apart, brands keep look-alike strings apart.
  • An untyped package gets @types/... or a small declaration of what you use, with real types; never an empty declare module.
  • Frameworks such as Next.js add server rendering and server components; React Native reuses your React, TypeScript, data layer and the shared core/types.ts.
  • Teams: short branches, pull requests with a template, CI and labeled, kind review (blocking, suggestion, nit); upgrades one major at a time after the release notes, with CI deciding.
Worked example: satisfies versus an annotation

satisfies checks a value and keeps its own type; an annotation replaces it. In advanced.ts, with satisfies, after changing title('impact') to title('volunteers'):

npm run check
advanced.ts:26:19 - error TS2345: Argument of type '"volunteers"' is not assignable to parameter of type '"events" | "impact" | "sites"'.

26 console.log(title('volunteers'))
                     ~~~~~~~~~~~~


Found 1 error in advanced.ts:26

With const pageTitles: Record<string, string> = { instead, the same line passes the check: the annotation threw the keys away, so any string is a page name. Delete a line of the satisfies table instead, and the check names it:

advanced.ts:23:3 - error TS2741: Property 'cancelled' is missing in type '{ draft: "neutral"; scheduled: "info"; completed: "success"; }' but required in type 'Record<EventStatus, "danger" | "info" | "neutral" | "success">'.
Found 1 error in advanced.ts:23

  • Task 1: as → satisfies for terrainTips (read the error, add marsh), then satisfies for reportTitles.
  • Tasks 2 and 3 at home: three types computed from types, a declaration file.

type EventStatus = 'draft' | 'scheduled' | 'completed' | 'cancelled'

// "as" tells TypeScript to trust you: the missing statuses go unnoticed
const tones = {
  draft: 'neutral',
  scheduled: 'info',
} as Record<EventStatus, string>

console.log(tones.completed.toUpperCase())

interface Settings {
  theme: 'system' | 'light' | 'dark'
  density: 'roomy' | 'compact'
}

type Setters<T> = {
  [K in keyof T & string as `set${Capitalize<K>}`]: (value: T[K]) => void
}

Glossary

TermMeaning
brief, charterThe client's request / your one-page answer: people, must-haves, nice later, out of scope (with reasons)
out of scopeWhat you deliberately will not build, and why: it protects you and the client
domain typesThe types that describe the client's world (RepairTicket, TuningVisit), written before any component
CentsMoney as a whole number of the smallest unit: sums never drift
computed vs stored valueA total or "overdue" worked out when needed, never saved where it can disagree with its data
mock APIMSW handlers and sample data that answer like a real server, in tests, dev:demo and the demo build
walking skeletonMilestone 2: the thinnest app from your types to a public draft address
milestone, time logA step with a "done when" / estimated and actual hours per milestone
rubric, peer reviewTen rows scored 0 / 1 / 2 (complete = no row at "not yet") / a classmate's 90-minute check of your live link and repository
satisfiesCheck a value against a type and keep the value's own, more precise type
mapped typeA type built from another type's keys: { [K in keyof T]: ... }, renamed with as
template literal typeA string type with a pattern: /api/${Collection}
conditional type, inferT extends U ? X : Y, with infer naming a part to pull out
branded typestring & { readonly __brand: 'EventId' }: a string TypeScript keeps apart from other strings
declaration file (.d.ts)Types for code that has none; @types/ packages come from DefinitelyTyped
GitHub flow, pull requestShort branches merged into main through a reviewed request with CI
blocking / suggestion / nitReview labels: must change / better, your call / tiny, style
